</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">Robert Anthony Eden</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1897-06-12</span>)</span>12 June 1897<br /><a href="/wiki/Rushyford" title="Rushyford">Rushyford</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">14 January 1977<span style="display:none">(1977-01-14)</span> (aged&#160;79)<br /><a href="/wiki/Alvediston" title="Alvediston">Alvediston</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label">St Mary's Churchyard, Alvediston</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouses</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist">

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Awards</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Military_Cross" title="Military Cross">Military Cross</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Sir Robert Anthony Eden, 1st Earl of Avon</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Military_Cross" title="Military Cross">MC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a></span> (12&#160;June 1897&#160;– 14 January 1977) was a British politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Leader of the Conservative Party (UK)">Leader of the Conservative Party</a> from 1955 until his resignation in 1957.
</p><p>Achieving rapid promotion as a young Conservative <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">member of Parliament</a>, he became foreign secretary aged 38, before resigning in protest at <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Appeasement_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="Appeasement policy">appeasement policy</a> towards <a href="/wiki/Benito_Mussolini" title="Benito Mussolini">Mussolini</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Fascist_Italy_(1922%E2%80%931943)" title="Fascist Italy (1922–1943)">Fascist regime in Italy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-wc1_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wc1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He again held that position for most of the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>, and a third time in the early 1950s. Having been deputy to <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> for almost 15 years, Eden succeeded him as the leader of the Conservative Party and prime minister in 1955, and a month later <a href="/wiki/1955_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1955 United Kingdom general election">won a general election</a>.
</p><p>Eden's reputation as a skilled diplomat was overshadowed in 1956 when the United States refused to support the Anglo-French military response to the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a>, which critics across party lines regarded as a historic setback for <a href="/wiki/British_foreign_policy" class="mw-redirect" title="British foreign policy">British foreign policy</a>, signalling the end of <a href="/wiki/British_Empire#Decolonisation_and_decline_(1945–1997)" title="British Empire">British influence in the Middle East</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Most historians argue that he made a series of blunders, especially not realising the depth of American opposition to military action.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Two months after ordering an end to the <a href="/wiki/Operation_Musketeer_(1956)" title="Operation Musketeer (1956)">Suez operation</a>, he resigned as Prime Minister on grounds of ill health, and because he was widely suspected of having misled the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> over the degree of collusion with <a href="/wiki/French_Fourth_Republic" title="French Fourth Republic">France</a> and <a href="/wiki/Israel" title="Israel">Israel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden is generally considered to be among the least successful <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom">of British prime ministers</a> in the 20th century, although two broadly sympathetic biographies have gone some way to shifting the balance of opinion.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RRJames_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RRJames-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Thorpe2003_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorpe2003-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He was the first out of fifteen British prime ministers to be appointed by <a href="/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Elizabeth II">Queen Elizabeth II</a> in her seventy-year reign.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Eden was born on 12 June 1897 at <a href="/wiki/Windlestone_Hall" title="Windlestone Hall">Windlestone Hall</a>, County Durham, into a conservative family of <a href="/wiki/Landed_gentry" title="Landed gentry">landed gentry</a>. He was the third of four sons of <a href="/wiki/Sir_William_Eden,_7th_Baronet" title="Sir William Eden, 7th Baronet">Sir William Eden, 7th and 5th Baronet</a>, and Sybil Frances Grey, a member of the prominent <a href="/wiki/Earl_Grey" title="Earl Grey">Grey family</a> of <a href="/wiki/Northumberland" title="Northumberland">Northumberland</a>. Sir William was a former colonel and local <a href="/wiki/Magistrate" title="Magistrate">magistrate</a> from an <a href="/wiki/Eden_baronets" title="Eden baronets">old titled family</a>. An eccentric and often foul-tempered man, he was a talented watercolourist, portraitist, and collector of <a href="/wiki/Impressionism" title="Impressionism">Impressionists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_9–14_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_9–14-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Eden's mother had wanted to marry <a href="/wiki/Francis_Knollys,_1st_Viscount_Knollys" title="Francis Knollys, 1st Viscount Knollys">Francis Knollys</a>, who later became a significant Royal adviser, but the match was forbidden by <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">the Prince of Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Although she was a popular figure locally, she had a strained relationship with her children, and her profligacy ruined the family fortunes,<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_9–14_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_9–14-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> meaning Eden's elder brother Tim had to sell Windlestone in 1936.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Referring to his parentage, <a href="/wiki/Rab_Butler" title="Rab Butler">Rab Butler</a> would later quip that Anthony Eden— a handsome but ill-tempered man— was "half mad baronet, half beautiful woman".<sup id="cite_ref-Thorpe2003_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorpe2003-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden's great-grandfather was William Iremonger, who commanded the <a href="/wiki/2nd_Regiment_of_Foot" class="mw-redirect" title="2nd Regiment of Foot">2nd Regiment of Foot</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_War" title="Peninsular War">Peninsular War</a> and fought under <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Wellington</a> (as he became) at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Vimeiro" title="Battle of Vimeiro">Vimeiro</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He was also descended from Governor <a href="/wiki/Sir_Robert_Eden,_1st_Baronet,_of_Maryland" title="Sir Robert Eden, 1st Baronet, of Maryland">Sir Robert Eden, 1st Baronet, of Maryland</a> and, through the <a href="/wiki/Baron_Baltimore" title="Baron Baltimore">Calvert family of Maryland</a>, he was connected to the ancient <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> aristocracy of the <a href="/wiki/Baron_Arundell_of_Wardour" title="Baron Arundell of Wardour">Arundell</a> and <a href="/wiki/House_of_Howard" class="mw-redirect" title="House of Howard">Howard</a> families (including the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Norfolk" title="Duke of Norfolk">Dukes of Norfolk</a>), as well as <a href="/wiki/Anglicanism" title="Anglicanism">Anglican</a> families including as the <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Carlisle" title="Earl of Carlisle">earls of Carlisle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Effingham" title="Earl of Effingham">Effingham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Suffolk" title="Earl of Suffolk">Suffolk</a>. The Calverts had converted to the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Established Church</a> early in the 18th century to regain the <a href="/wiki/Proprietary_colony" title="Proprietary colony">proprietorship</a> of <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Maryland" title="Province of Maryland">Maryland</a>. He also had some Danish (the Schaffalitzky de Muckadell family) and Norwegian (the Bie family) descent.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Eden was once amused to learn that one of his ancestors had, like Churchill's ancestor <a href="/wiki/John_Churchill,_1st_Duke_of_Marlborough" title="John Churchill, 1st Duke of Marlborough">the Duke of Marlborough</a>, been the lover of <a href="/wiki/Barbara_Palmer,_1st_Duchess_of_Cleveland" title="Barbara Palmer, 1st Duchess of Cleveland">Barbara Castlemaine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was speculation for many years that Eden's biological father was the politician and man of letters <a href="/wiki/George_Wyndham" title="George Wyndham">George Wyndham</a>, but this is considered impossible as Wyndham was in South Africa at the time of Eden's conception.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Eden's mother was rumoured to have had an affair with Wyndham.<sup id="cite_ref-Thorpe2003_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorpe2003-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> His mother and Wyndham exchanged affectionate communications in 1896 but Wyndham was an infrequent visitor to Windlestone and probably did not reciprocate Sybil's feelings. Eden was amused by the rumours but, according to his biographer Rhodes James, probably did not believe them. He did not resemble his siblings, but his father Sir William attributed this to his being "a Grey, not an Eden".<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden had an elder brother, John, who was killed in action in 1914,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and a younger brother, Nicholas, who was killed when the <a href="/wiki/Battlecruiser" title="Battlecruiser">battlecruiser</a> <a href="/wiki/HMS_Indefatigable_(1909)" title="HMS Indefatigable (1909)">HMS&#160;<i>Indefatigable</i></a> blew up and sank at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Jutland" title="Battle of Jutland">Battle of Jutland</a> in 1916.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="School">School</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: School">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Eden was educated at two independent schools. He attended <a href="/wiki/Sandroyd_School" title="Sandroyd School">Sandroyd School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Wiltshire" title="Wiltshire">Wiltshire</a> from 1907 to 1910, where he excelled in languages.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> He then started at <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a> in January 1911.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> There, he won a <a href="/wiki/Divinity_(academic_discipline)" title="Divinity (academic discipline)">Divinity</a> prize and excelled at cricket, rugby and rowing, winning House <a href="/wiki/Colours,_standards_and_guidons" class="mw-redirect" title="Colours, standards and guidons">colours</a> in the last.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden learned French and German on continental holidays and, as a child, is said to have spoken French better than English.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Although Eden was able to converse with <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a> in German in February 1934 and with Chinese Premier <a href="/wiki/Zhou_Enlai" title="Zhou Enlai">Zhou Enlai</a> in French at Geneva in 1954, he preferred, out of a sense of professionalism, to have interpreters translate at formal meetings.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although Eden later claimed to have had no interest in politics until the early 1920s, his biographer writes that his teenage letters and diaries "only really come to life" when discussing the subject. He was a strong, partisan Conservative, thinking his <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protectionist</a> father "a fool" in November 1912 for trying to block his free-trade supporting uncle from a Parliamentary candidacy. He rejoiced in the defeat of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Masterman" title="Charles Masterman">Charles Masterman</a> at <a href="/wiki/1914_Ipswich_by-election" title="1914 Ipswich by-election">a by-election in May 1914</a><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and once astonished his mother on a train journey by telling her the MP and the size of his majority for each constituency through which they passed.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> By 1914 he was a member of the Eton Society ("Pop").<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_World_War">First World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: First World War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">First World War</a>, Eden's elder brother, Lieutenant John Eden, was killed in action on 17 October 1914, at the age of 26, while serving with the <a href="/wiki/12th_Royal_Lancers" title="12th Royal Lancers">12th (Prince of Wales's Royal) Lancers</a>. He is buried in <a href="/wiki/Larch_Wood_(Railway_Cutting)_Commonwealth_War_Graves_Commission_Cemetery" title="Larch Wood (Railway Cutting) Commonwealth War Graves Commission Cemetery">Larch Wood (Railway Cutting) Commonwealth War Graves Commission Cemetery</a> in Belgium.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> His uncle Robin was later shot down and captured whilst serving with the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Flying_Corps" title="Royal Flying Corps">Royal Flying Corps</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Volunteering for service in the <a href="/wiki/British_Army" title="British Army">British Army</a>, like many others of his generation, Eden served with the 21st (Yeoman Rifles) Battalion of the <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Royal_Rifle_Corps" title="King&#39;s Royal Rifle Corps">King's Royal Rifle Corps</a> (KRRC), a <a href="/wiki/Kitchener%27s_Army" title="Kitchener&#39;s Army">Kitchener's Army</a> unit, initially recruited mainly from <a href="/wiki/County_Durham" title="County Durham">County Durham</a> country labourers, who were increasingly replaced by Londoners after losses at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Somme" title="Battle of the Somme">Somme</a> in mid-1916.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He was commissioned as a temporary <a href="/wiki/Second_lieutenant" title="Second lieutenant">second lieutenant</a> on 2 November 1915 (antedated to 29 September 1915).<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> His battalion transferred to the <a href="/wiki/Western_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Western Front (World War I)">Western Front</a> on 4 May 1916 as part of the <a href="/wiki/41st_Division_(United_Kingdom)" title="41st Division (United Kingdom)">41st Division</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> On 31 May 1916, Eden's younger brother, Midshipman William Nicholas Eden, was killed in action, aged 16, on board <a href="/wiki/HMS_Indefatigable_(1909)" title="HMS Indefatigable (1909)">HMS&#160;<i>Indefatigable</i></a> during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Jutland" title="Battle of Jutland">Battle of Jutland</a>. He is commemorated on the <a href="/wiki/Plymouth_Naval_Memorial" title="Plymouth Naval Memorial">Plymouth Naval Memorial</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> His brother-in-law, Lord Brooke, was wounded during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One summer night in 1916, near <a href="/wiki/Ploegsteert" title="Ploegsteert">Ploegsteert</a>, Eden had to lead a small raid into an enemy trench to kill or capture enemy soldiers to identify the enemy units opposite. He and his men were pinned down in <a href="/wiki/No_man%27s_land" title="No man&#39;s land">no man's land</a> under enemy fire, his sergeant seriously wounded in the leg. Eden sent one man back to British lines to fetch another man and a stretcher, and he and three others carried the wounded sergeant back with, as he later put it in his memoirs, a "chilly feeling down our spines", unsure whether the Germans had not seen them in the dark or were chivalrously declining to fire. He omitted to mention that he had been awarded the <a href="/wiki/Military_Cross" title="Military Cross">Military Cross</a> (MC) for the incident, of which he  made little mention in his political career.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> On 18 September 1916, after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Flers-Courcelette" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Flers-Courcelette">Battle of Flers-Courcelette</a> (part of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Somme" title="Battle of the Somme">Battle of the Somme</a>), he wrote to his mother, "I have seen things lately that I am not likely to forget".<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> On 3 October, he was appointed an <a href="/wiki/Adjutant#Military_and_paramilitary_appointment#Britain_and_the_Commonwealth" title="Adjutant">adjutant</a>, with the rank of temporary <a href="/wiki/Lieutenant_(British_Army_and_Royal_Marines)" title="Lieutenant (British Army and Royal Marines)">lieutenant</a> for the duration of that appointment.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> At the age of 19, he was the youngest adjutant on the Western Front.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden's MC was gazetted in the <a href="/wiki/1917_Birthday_Honours" title="1917 Birthday Honours">1917 Birthday Honours</a> list.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> His battalion fought at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Messines_(1917)" title="Battle of Messines (1917)">Messines Ridge</a> in June 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> On 1 July 1917, Eden was confirmed as a temporary lieutenant,<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> relinquishing his appointment as adjutant three days later.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> His battalion fought in the first few days of <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Passchendaele" title="Battle of Passchendaele">Third Battle of Ypres</a> (31 July – 4 August).<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Between 20 and 23 September 1917 his battalion spent a few days on coastal defence on the Franco-Belgian border.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 19 November, Eden was transferred to the General Staff as a <a href="/wiki/Staff_(military)" title="Staff (military)">General Staff Officer Grade 3</a> (GSO3), with the temporary rank of <a href="/wiki/Captain_(British_Army_and_Royal_Marines)" title="Captain (British Army and Royal Marines)">captain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> He served at <a href="/wiki/Second_Army_(United_Kingdom)" title="Second Army (United Kingdom)">Second Army</a> HQ between mid-November 1917 and 8 March 1918, missing out on <a href="/wiki/Italian_Expeditionary_Force" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian Expeditionary Force">service in Italy</a> (as the 41st Division had been transferred <a href="/wiki/Italian_Front_(World_War_I)" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian Front (World War I)">there</a> after the <a href="/wiki/Second_Army_(Italy)" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Army (Italy)">Italian Second Army</a> was defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Caporetto" title="Battle of Caporetto">Battle of Caporetto</a>). Eden returned to the Western Front as a <a href="/wiki/Operation_Michael" title="Operation Michael">major German offensive</a> was clearly imminent, only for his former battalion to be disbanded to help alleviate the British Army's acute manpower shortage.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Although <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>, then the <a href="/wiki/British_prime_minister" class="mw-redirect" title="British prime minister">British prime minister</a>, was one of the few politicians of whom Eden reported frontline soldiers speaking highly, he wrote to his sister (23 December 1917) in disgust at his "wait and see twaddle" in declining to extend <a href="/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1918" title="Conscription Crisis of 1918">conscription to Ireland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.52_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.52-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1918, during the <a href="/wiki/German_spring_offensive" title="German spring offensive">German spring offensive</a>, he was stationed near <a href="/wiki/La_F%C3%A8re" title="La Fère">La Fère</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Oise_(river)" title="Oise (river)">Oise</a>, opposite <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>, as he learned at a conference in 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> At one point, when brigade HQ was bombed by German aircraft, his companion told him, "There now, you have had your first taste of the next war."<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> On 26 May 1918, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Brigade_major" title="Brigade major">brigade major</a> of the <a href="/wiki/198th_(East_Lancashire)_Brigade" title="198th (East Lancashire) Brigade">198th Infantry Brigade</a>, part of the <a href="/wiki/66th_Division_(United_Kingdom)" class="mw-redirect" title="66th Division (United Kingdom)">66th Division</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.52_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.52-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> At the age of 20, Eden was the youngest brigade major in the British Army.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He considered standing for Parliament at the end of the war, but the <a href="/wiki/1918_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1918 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> was called too early for that to be possible.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55_44-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.55-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> After the <a href="/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918" title="Armistice of 11 November 1918">Armistice with Germany</a>, he spent the winter of 1918–1919 in the Ardennes with his brigade; on 28 March 1919, he transferred to be brigade major of the <a href="/wiki/99th_Brigade_(United_Kingdom)" title="99th Brigade (United Kingdom)">99th Infantry Brigade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Eden contemplated applying for a commission in the Regular Army, but these were very hard to come by with the army contracting so rapidly. He initially shrugged off his mother's suggestion of studying at Oxford. He also rejected the thought of becoming a <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barrister</a>. His preferred career alternatives at this stage were standing for Parliament for Bishop Auckland, the Civil Service in East Africa or the Foreign Office.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He was demobilised on 13 June 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8_32-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._5–8-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He retained the rank of captain.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Oxford">Oxford</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Oxford">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Uffizi_Society,_Oxford.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/66/The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg/220px-The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/66/The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg/330px-The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/66/The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg/440px-The_Uffizi_Society%2C_Oxford.jpg 2x" data-file-width="970" data-file-height="636" /></a><figcaption>The Uffizi Society Oxford, ca. 1920. First row standing: later Sir Henry Studholme (5th from left). Seated: Lord Balniel, later 28th Earl of Crawford (2nd from left); Ralph Dutton, later 8th Baron Sherborne (3rd from left); Anthony Eden, later Earl of Avon (4th from left); Lord David Cecil (5th from left).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Eden had dabbled in the study of <a href="/wiki/Turkish_language" title="Turkish language">Turkish</a> with a family friend.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> After the war, he studied <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Languages" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Languages">Oriental Languages</a> (<a href="/wiki/Persian_language" title="Persian language">Persian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a>) at <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>, starting in October 1919.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Persian was his main and Arabic his secondary language. He studied under Richard Paset Dewhurst and <a href="/wiki/David_Samuel_Margoliouth" title="David Samuel Margoliouth">David Samuel Margoliouth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At Oxford, Eden took no part in student politics, and his main leisure interest at the time was art.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Eden was in the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_Dramatic_Society" title="Oxford University Dramatic Society">Oxford University Dramatic Society</a> and President of the Asiatic Society. Along with <a href="/wiki/Lord_David_Cecil" title="Lord David Cecil">Lord David Cecil</a> and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gathorne-Hardy" title="Robert Gathorne-Hardy">R. E. Gathorne-Hardy</a> he founded the Uffizi Society, of which he later became president. Possibly under the influence of his father, Eden gave a paper on <a href="/wiki/Paul_C%C3%A9zanne" title="Paul Cézanne">Paul Cézanne</a>, whose work was not yet widely appreciated.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._8–9-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Eden was already collecting paintings.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1920, still an undergraduate, Eden was recalled to military service as a lieutenant in the 6th Battalion of the <a href="/wiki/Durham_Light_Infantry" title="Durham Light Infantry">Durham Light Infantry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In the spring of 1921, once again as a temporary captain, he commanded local defence forces at <a href="/wiki/Spennymoor" title="Spennymoor">Spennymoor</a> as <a href="/wiki/Triple_Alliance_(1914)#The_Post-War_Triple_Alliance" title="Triple Alliance (1914)">serious industrial unrest</a> seemed possible.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.62_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.62-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He again relinquished his commission on 8 July.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He graduated from Oxford in June 1922 with a <a href="/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification#Variations_of_First_Class_honours" title="British undergraduate degree classification">Double First</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62_50-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp._59–62-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> He continued to serve as an officer in the Territorial Army until May 1923.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_political_career.2C_1922.E2.80.931931"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career,_1922–1931">Early political career, 1922–1931</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Early political career, 1922–1931">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="1922.E2.80.931924"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1922–1924">1922–1924</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: 1922–1924">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Captain Eden, as he was still known, was selected to contest <a href="/wiki/Spennymoor_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Spennymoor (UK Parliament constituency)">Spennymoor</a>, as a <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a>. At first, he had hoped to win with some <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> support as the Conservatives were still supporting <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_George_ministry" title="Lloyd George ministry">Lloyd George's coalition government</a>, but by the time of the <a href="/wiki/1922_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1922 United Kingdom general election">November 1922 general election</a>, it was clear that the surge in the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> vote made that unlikely.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> His main sponsor was the <a href="/wiki/Charles_Vane-Tempest-Stewart,_7th_Marquess_of_Londonderry" title="Charles Vane-Tempest-Stewart, 7th Marquess of Londonderry">Marquess of Londonderry</a>, a local coal owner. The seat went from Liberal to Labour.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden's father had died on 20 February 1915.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> As a younger son, he had inherited capital of £7,675 and in 1922 he had a private income of £706 after tax (approximately £375,000 and £35,000 at 2014 prices).<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.62_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p.62-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-measuringworth.com_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-measuringworth.com-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden read the writings of <a href="/wiki/George_Curzon,_1st_Marquess_Curzon_of_Kedleston" title="George Curzon, 1st Marquess Curzon of Kedleston">Lord Curzon</a> and was hoping to emulate him by entering politics with a view to specialising in foreign affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Eden married <a href="/wiki/Beatrice_Beckett" title="Beatrice Beckett">Beatrice Beckett</a> in the autumn of 1923, and after a two-day honeymoon in Essex, he was selected to fight <a href="/wiki/Warwick_and_Leamington_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Warwick and Leamington (UK Parliament constituency)">Warwick and Leamington</a> for a by-election in November 1923. His Labour opponent, <a href="/wiki/Daisy_Greville,_Countess_of_Warwick" title="Daisy Greville, Countess of Warwick">Daisy Greville</a>, Countess of Warwick, was by coincidence his sister Elfrida's mother-in-law and also mother to his wife's step-mother, Marjorie Blanche Eve Beckett, née Greville.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> On 16 November 1923, during the by-election campaign, Parliament was dissolved for the <a href="/wiki/1923_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1923 United Kingdom general election">December 1923 general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> He was elected to Parliament at the age of twenty-six.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_78–9_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_78–9-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_MacDonald_ministry" title="First MacDonald ministry">first Labour Government</a>, under <a href="/wiki/Ramsay_MacDonald" title="Ramsay MacDonald">Ramsay MacDonald</a>, took office in January 1924. Eden's <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> (19 February 1924) was a controversial attack on Labour's defence policy and was heckled, and he was thereafter careful to speak only after deep preparation.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_78–9_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_78–9-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> He later reprinted the speech in the collection <i>Foreign Affairs</i> (1939) to give an impression that he had been a consistent advocate of air strength. Eden admired <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a>, then in his final year in the Commons, for his lucidity and brevity. On 1 April 1924, he spoke to urge <a href="/wiki/Turkey%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations" title="Turkey–United Kingdom relations">Anglo-Turkish friendship</a> and the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne" title="Treaty of Lausanne">Treaty of Lausanne</a>, which had been signed in July 1923.<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._12–13_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._12–13-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1924.E2.80.931929"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1924–1929">1924–1929</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1924–1929">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Conservatives returned to power at the <a href="/wiki/1924_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1924 United Kingdom general election">1924 General Election</a>. In January 1925, Eden, disappointed not to have been offered a position, went on a tour of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_East" title="Middle East">Middle East</a> and met <a href="/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq" title="Faisal I of Iraq">Emir Feisal of Iraq</a>. Feisal reminded him of the "<a href="/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia" title="Nicholas II of Russia">Czar of Russia</a> &amp; (I) suspect that his fate may be similar" (a similar fate indeed befell the Iraqi Royal Family <a href="/wiki/14_July_Revolution" title="14 July Revolution">in 1958</a>). During a visit to <a href="/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran" title="Pahlavi Iran">Pahlavi Iran</a> he inspected the <a href="/wiki/Abadan_Refinery" title="Abadan Refinery">Abadan Refinery</a>, which he likened to "a <a href="/wiki/Swansea" title="Swansea">Swansea</a> on a small scale".<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_84–6_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_84–6-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was appointed <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Private_Secretary" title="Parliamentary Private Secretary">Parliamentary Private Secretary</a> to <a href="/wiki/Godfrey_Locker-Lampson" title="Godfrey Locker-Lampson">Godfrey Locker-Lampson</a>, Under-Secretary at the Home Office (17 February 1925), serving under Home Secretary <a href="/wiki/William_Joynson_Hicks" class="mw-redirect" title="William Joynson Hicks">William Joynson Hicks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p85_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p85-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1925, he went on a second trip to Canada, Australia and India.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_84–6_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_84–6-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> He wrote articles for <i><a href="/wiki/The_Yorkshire_Post" title="The Yorkshire Post">The Yorkshire Post</a></i>, controlled by his father-in-law Sir <a href="/wiki/Gervase_Beckett" title="Gervase Beckett">Gervase Beckett</a>, under the pseudonym "Backbencher".<sup id="cite_ref-Aster_1976,_pp._12–13_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aster_1976,_pp._12–13-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> In September 1925, he represented the <i>Yorkshire Post</i> at the Imperial Conference at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden continued to be PPS to Locker-Lampson when the latter was appointed Under-Secretary at the Foreign Office in December 1925.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p85_66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p85-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> He distinguished himself with a speech on the Middle East (21 December 1925),<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> that called for the readjustment of Iraqi frontiers in favour of Turkey but also for a continued <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_Iraq" title="Mandatory Iraq">British mandate</a>, rather than a "scuttle". Eden ended his speech by calling for Anglo-Turkish friendship. On 23 March 1926, he spoke to urge the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> to admit Germany, which would happen the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> In July 1926 he became PPS to the Foreign Secretary Sir <a href="/wiki/Austen_Chamberlain" title="Austen Chamberlain">Austen Chamberlain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Besides supplementing his parliamentary income of around £300 a year at that time by writing and journalism, he published a book about his travels, <i>Places in the Sun</i> in 1926 that was highly critical of the detrimental effect of socialism on Australia and to which <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> wrote a foreword.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p103_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p103-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1928, with Austen Chamberlain away on a voyage to recover his health, Eden had to speak for the government in a debate on a recent Anglo-French naval agreement in reply to Ramsay MacDonald, then Leader of the Opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> According to Austen Chamberlain, he would have been promoted to his first ministerial job, Under-Secretary at the Foreign Office, if the Conservatives had won the <a href="/wiki/1929_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1929 United Kingdom general election">1929 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p101_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p101-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1929.E2.80.931931"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1929–1931">1929–1931</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: 1929–1931">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The 1929 general election was the only time that Eden received less than 50% of the vote at Warwick.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> After the Conservative defeat, he joined a progressive group of younger politicians consisting of <a href="/wiki/Oliver_Stanley" title="Oliver Stanley">Oliver Stanley</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Ormsby-Gore,_4th_Baron_Harlech" title="William Ormsby-Gore, 4th Baron Harlech">William Ormsby-Gore</a> and the future <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_House_of_Commons_(United_Kingdom)" title="Speaker of the House of Commons (United Kingdom)">Speaker</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Morrison,_1st_Viscount_Dunrossil" title="William Morrison, 1st Viscount Dunrossil">W.S. "Shakes" Morrison</a>. Another member was <a href="/wiki/Noel_Skelton" title="Noel Skelton">Noel Skelton</a>, who had before his death coined the phrase "property-owning democracy", which Eden was later to popularise as a Conservative Party aspiration. Eden advocated co-partnership in industry between managers and workers, whom he wanted to be given <a href="/wiki/Share_(finance)" title="Share (finance)">shares</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p101_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p101-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In opposition between 1929 and 1931, Eden worked as a City broker for Harry Lucas, a firm that was eventually absorbed into <a href="/wiki/S._G._Warburg_%26_Co." title="S. G. Warburg &amp; Co.">S. G. Warburg &amp; Co.</a><sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p103_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p103-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Foreign_Affairs_Minister.2C_1931.E2.80.931935"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Affairs_Minister,_1931–1935">Foreign Affairs Minister, 1931–1935</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Foreign Affairs Minister, 1931–1935">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In August 1931, Eden held his first ministerial office as <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Under-Secretary_of_State" title="Parliamentary Under-Secretary of State">Under-Secretary</a> for <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy" title="Foreign policy">Foreign Affairs</a> in Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Ramsay_MacDonald" title="Ramsay MacDonald">Ramsay MacDonald</a>'s <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(United_Kingdom)" title="National Government (United Kingdom)">National Government</a>. Initially, the office was held by <a href="/wiki/Rufus_Isaacs,_1st_Marquess_of_Reading" title="Rufus Isaacs, 1st Marquess of Reading">Lord Reading</a> (in the House of Lords), but <a href="/wiki/John_Simon,_1st_Viscount_Simon" title="John Simon, 1st Viscount Simon">Sir John Simon</a> held the position from November 1931.
</p><p>Like many of his generation who had served in the First World War, Eden was strongly <a href="/wiki/Antiwar" class="mw-redirect" title="Antiwar">antiwar</a>, and he strove to work through the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> to preserve European peace. The government proposed <a href="/wiki/World_Disarmament_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="World Disarmament Conference">measures</a> superseding the post-war <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Versailles Treaty</a> to allow Germany to rearm (albeit replacing its <a href="/wiki/Reichswehr" title="Reichswehr">small professional army</a> with a short-service militia) and to reduce French armaments. <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> criticised the policy sharply in the House of Commons on 23 March 1933, opposing "undue" French disarmament as this might require Britain to take action to enforce peace under the 1925 <a href="/wiki/Locarno_Treaties" title="Locarno Treaties">Locarno Treaty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-wc1_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wc1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Eden, replying for the government, dismissed Churchill's speech as exaggerated and unconstructive and commented that land disarmament had yet to make the same progress as naval disarmament at the <a href="/wiki/Washington_Naval_Conference" title="Washington Naval Conference">Washington</a> and <a href="/wiki/London_Naval_Treaty" title="London Naval Treaty">London</a> Treaties and arguing that French disarmament was needed to "secure for Europe that period of appeasement which is needed".<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Eden's speech was met with approval by the House of Commons. <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> commented shortly afterwards, "That young man is coming along rapidly; not only can he make a good speech but he has a good head and what advice he gives is listened to by the Cabinet".<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Eden later wrote that in the early 1930s, the word "appeasement" was still used in its correct sense (from the <i><a href="/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary" title="Oxford English Dictionary">Oxford English Dictionary</a></i>) of seeking to settle strife. Only later in the decade would it come to acquire a pejorative meaning of acceding to bullying demands.<sup id="cite_ref-wc1_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wc1-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was appointed <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a> in December 1933,<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> a position that was combined with the newly created office of Minister for League of Nations Affairs. As Lord Privy Seal, Eden was sworn of the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1934_Birthday_Honours" title="1934 Birthday Honours">1934 Birthday Honours</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>On 25 March 1935, accompanying <a href="/wiki/Sir_John_Simon" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir John Simon">Sir John Simon</a>, Eden met Hitler in Berlin and raised a weak protest after Hitler restored conscription against the Versailles Treaty. The same month, Eden also met <a href="/wiki/Stalin" class="mw-redirect" title="Stalin">Stalin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maxim_Litvinov" title="Maxim Litvinov">Litvinov</a> in Moscow.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He entered the cabinet for the first time when <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> formed his third administration in June 1935. Eden later came to recognise that peace could not be maintained by appeasement of <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy_under_Fascism_(1922%E2%80%931943)" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Italy under Fascism (1922–1943)">Fascist Italy</a>. He privately opposed the policy of the Foreign Secretary, Sir <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Hoare,_1st_Viscount_Templewood" title="Samuel Hoare, 1st Viscount Templewood">Samuel Hoare</a>, of trying to appease Italy during its <a href="/wiki/Second_Italo-Abyssinian_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Italo-Abyssinian War">invasion of Abyssinia</a> (now called <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopia</a>) in 1935. After Hoare resigned after the failure of the <a href="/wiki/Hoare-Laval_Pact" class="mw-redirect" title="Hoare-Laval Pact">Hoare-Laval Pact</a>, Eden succeeded him as Foreign Secretary. When Eden had his first audience with <a href="/wiki/King_George_V" class="mw-redirect" title="King George V">King George&#160;V</a>, the King is said to have remarked, "No more coals to Newcastle, no more Hoares to Paris".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1935, Baldwin sent Eden on a two-day visit to see Hitler, with whom he dined twice.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Litvinov's biographer John Holroyd-Doveton believed that Eden shares with Molotov the experience of being the only people to have had dinner with Hitler, Churchill, Roosevelt and Stalin although not on the same occasion. Hitler never had dinner with any of the other three leaders, and as far as is known, Stalin never saw Hitler.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<p><a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Attlee</a> was convinced that public opinion could stop Hitler, saying in a speech in the House of Commons:</p><blockquote><p>"We believe in a League system in which the whole world would be ranged against an aggressor. If it is shown that someone is proposing to break the peace let us bring the whole world opinion against her".<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>However, Eden was more realistic and correctly predicted:</p><blockquote><p>"Hitler could only be stopped. There may be the only course of action open to us to join with those powers who are members of the League in affirming our faith in that institution and to uphold the principles of the <a href="/wiki/Covenant_of_the_League_of_Nations" title="Covenant of the League of Nations">Covenant</a>. It may be the spectacle of the great powers of the League reaffirming their intentions to collaborate more closely than ever is not only the sole means of bringing home to Germany that the inevitable effect of persisting in her present policy will be to consolidate against her all those nations which believe in collective security but will also tend to give confidence to those less powerful nations which through fear of Germany's growing strength might well otherwise be drawn into her orbit".<sup id="cite_ref-auto_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>Eden proceeded to Moscow for talks with Stalin and Soviet Minister Litvinov,<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Most of the British cabinet feared of the spread of Bolshevism to Britain and hated the Soviets, but Eden went with an open mind and had a respect for Stalin:</p><blockquote><p>"(Stalin's) personality made itself felt without exaggeration. He had natural good manners, perhaps a Georgian inheritance. Though I knew the man was without mercy, I respected the quality of his mind and even felt a sympathy I have never been able to analyse. Perhaps it was because of the pragmatic approach. I cannot believe he had any affinity to <a href="/wiki/Karl_Marx" title="Karl Marx">Marx</a>. Certainly no one could have been less doctrinaire".<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>Eden felt sure most of his colleagues would feel unenthusiastic about any favourable report on the Soviet Union but felt certain to be correct.
</p><p>The representatives of both governments were happy to note that as a result of a full and frank exchange of views, there is at present no conflict of interest between them on any of the major issues of international policy, which provided a firm foundation between them in the cause of peace. Eden stated when he sent the communiqué to his government, he thought that his colleagues would be "Unenthusiastic, I am sure".<sup id="cite_ref-auto_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>John Holroyd-Doveton argued that Eden would be proved right. Not only was the French army defeated by the German army, but France broke its treaty with Britain by seeking an armistice with Germany. In contrast, the Red Army finally defeated the <a href="/wiki/Wehrmacht" title="Wehrmacht">Wehrmacht</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At that stage in his career, Eden was considered as something of a leader of fashion. He regularly wore a <a href="/wiki/Homburg_(hat)" class="mw-redirect" title="Homburg (hat)">Homburg</a> hat, which became known in Britain as an "<a href="/wiki/Anthony_Eden_hat" title="Anthony Eden hat">Anthony Eden</a>".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Foreign_Secretary_and_resignation.2C_1935.E2.80.931938"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary_and_resignation,_1935–1938">Foreign Secretary and resignation, 1935–1938</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary and resignation, 1935–1938">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:L%C3%A9on_Blum,_Anthony_Eden,_1936.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg/170px-L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg/255px-L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg/340px-L%C3%A9on_Blum%2C_Anthony_Eden%2C_1936.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4453" data-file-height="4974" /></a><figcaption>Eden with French Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Blum" title="Léon Blum">Léon Blum</a> in Geneva in 1936</figcaption></figure>
<p>Eden became Foreign Secretary after <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Hoare,_1st_Viscount_Templewood" title="Samuel Hoare, 1st Viscount Templewood">Samuel Hoare</a> had resigned (→ <a href="/wiki/Hoare%E2%80%93Laval_Pact" title="Hoare–Laval Pact">Hoare–Laval Pact</a>). Britain had to adjust its <a href="/wiki/Foreign_policy" title="Foreign policy">foreign policy</a> to face the rise of the fascist powers (<a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a> / <a href="/wiki/Hitler" class="mw-redirect" title="Hitler">Hitler</a> and <a href="/wiki/Italian_fascism" title="Italian fascism">Italian fascism</a> / <a href="/wiki/Mussolini" class="mw-redirect" title="Mussolini">Mussolini</a>). He supported the policy of non-interference in the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War" title="Spanish Civil War">Spanish Civil War</a> through conferences such as the <a href="/wiki/Nyon_Conference" title="Nyon Conference">Nyon Conference</a> and supported <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain#Premiership_(1937–1940)" title="Neville Chamberlain">Prime Minister Neville Chamberlain</a> and <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(1937%E2%80%931939)" title="National Government (1937–1939)">his National Government</a> in their <a href="/wiki/European_foreign_policy_of_the_Chamberlain_ministry" title="European foreign policy of the Chamberlain ministry">efforts to preserve peace</a> through reasonable concessions to <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Second_Italo-Ethiopian_War" title="Second Italo-Ethiopian War">Italian-Ethiopian War</a> was brewing, and Eden tried in vain to persuade Mussolini to submit the dispute to the League of Nations. The Italian dictator scoffed at Eden publicly as "the best dressed fool in Europe". Eden did not protest when Britain and France failed to oppose Hitler's <a href="/wiki/Remilitarisation_of_the_Rhineland" class="mw-redirect" title="Remilitarisation of the Rhineland">reoccupation of the Rhineland</a> on 7 March 1936. When the French government (<a href="/wiki/Albert_Sarraut#Sarraut&#39;s_Second_Ministry,_24_January_–_4_June_1936" title="Albert Sarraut">Sarraut II government</a>) requested a meeting with a view to some kind of military action in response to Hitler's occupation, Eden's statement firmly ruled out any military assistance to France.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden resigned on 20 February 1938 as a public protest against Chamberlain's policy of coming to friendly terms with <a href="/wiki/Fascist_Italy_(1922%E2%80%931943)" title="Fascist Italy (1922–1943)">Fascist Italy</a>. Eden used secret intelligence reports to conclude that the Mussolini regime in Italy posed a threat to Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden still had no complaints about the appeasement of Nazi Germany. He became a Conservative dissenter, leading a group that Conservative whip <a href="/wiki/David_Margesson" class="mw-redirect" title="David Margesson">David Margesson</a> called the "Glamour Boys". Meanwhile, the leading anti-appeaser <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> led a similar group, "The Old Guard".<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> They were not yet allies and would not see eye-to-eye until Churchill became prime minister in 1940. There was much speculation that Eden would become a rallying point for all the disparate opponents of Chamberlain, but Eden's position declined heavily among politicians since he maintained a low profile and avoided confrontation though he opposed the <a href="/wiki/Munich_Agreement" title="Munich Agreement">Munich Agreement</a> and abstained in the vote on it in the House of Commons. However, he remained popular in the country at large and, in later years, was often wrongly supposed to have resigned as Foreign Secretary in protest at the Munich Agreement and appeasement generally. In a 1967 interview, Eden explained his decision to resign: "we had an agreement with Mussolini about the Mediterranean and Spain, which he was violating by sending troops to Spain, and Chamberlain wanted to have another agreement. I thought Mussolini should honour the first one before we negotiated for the second. I was trying to fight a delaying action for Britain, and I could not go along with Chamberlain's policy".<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_World_War">Second World War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Second World War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg/220px-FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg/330px-FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg/440px-FDR_and_Anthony_Eden_at_the_Quebec_Conference.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="478" /></a><figcaption>Eden with <a href="/wiki/William_Lyon_Mackenzie_King" title="William Lyon Mackenzie King">Mackenzie King</a> and <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> meeting <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1943" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec Conference, 1943">Quebec Conference</a> in 1943.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016,_Potsdamer_Konferenz,_Molotow,_Byrnes,_Eden.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg/170px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="246" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg/255px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg/340px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-14059-0016%2C_Potsdamer_Konferenz%2C_Molotow%2C_Byrnes%2C_Eden.jpg 2x" data-file-width="505" data-file-height="730" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Potsdam_Conference" title="Potsdam Conference">Potsdam Conference</a>: The Foreign Ministers <a href="/wiki/Vyacheslav_Molotov" title="Vyacheslav Molotov">Vyacheslav Molotov</a>, <a href="/wiki/James_F._Byrnes" title="James F. Byrnes">James F. Byrnes</a> and Anthony Eden, July 1945.</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/1939_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="1939 in the United Kingdom">last months of peace in 1939</a>, Eden joined the <a href="/wiki/Army_Reserve_(United_Kingdom)" title="Army Reserve (United Kingdom)">Territorial Army</a> with the rank of major, in the London Rangers motorised battalion of the <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Royal_Rifle_Corps" title="King&#39;s Royal Rifle Corps">King's Royal Rifle Corps</a> and was at annual camp with them in <a href="/wiki/Beaulieu,_Hampshire" title="Beaulieu, Hampshire">Beaulieu, Hampshire</a>, when he heard news of the <a href="/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact" title="Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact">Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two days after the <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Poland" title="Invasion of Poland">outbreak of war</a>, on 3 September 1939, Eden, unlike most Territorials, did not mobilise for active service. Instead, he returned to Chamberlain's government as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Dominion_Affairs" title="Secretary of State for Dominion Affairs">Secretary of State for Dominion Affairs</a> and he visited <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine" title="Mandatory Palestine">Mandatory Palestine</a> in February 1940 to inspect the <a href="/wiki/Second_Australian_Imperial_Force" title="Second Australian Imperial Force">Second Australian Imperial Force</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> However, he was not in the <a href="/wiki/War_Cabinet" class="mw-redirect" title="War Cabinet">War Cabinet</a>. As a result, he was not a candidate for prime minister when Chamberlain resigned on 10 May 1940 after the <a href="/wiki/Norway_Debate" title="Norway Debate">Narvik Debate</a> and Churchill became prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Churchill appointed Eden <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War" title="Secretary of State for War">Secretary of State for War</a>.
</p><p>At the end of 1940, Eden returned to the <a href="/wiki/Foreign,_Commonwealth_and_Development_Office" title="Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office">Foreign Office</a> and became a member of the executive committee of the <a href="/wiki/Political_Warfare_Executive" title="Political Warfare Executive">Political Warfare Executive</a> in 1941. Although he was one of Churchill's closest confidants, his role in wartime was restricted because Churchill himself conducted the most important negotiations, those with <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a> and <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Stalin" title="Joseph Stalin">Joseph Stalin</a>, but Eden served loyally as Churchill's lieutenant.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In December 1941, he travelled by ship to the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a><sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> where he met the Soviet leader Stalin<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> and surveyed the battlefields upon which the Soviets had successfully defended Moscow from the German Army attack in <a href="/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa" title="Operation Barbarossa">Operation Barbarossa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nevertheless, he was in charge of handling most of the relations between Britain and the <a href="/wiki/Free_France" title="Free France">Free French</a> leader, <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a>, during the last years of the war. Eden was often both critical of the emphasis Churchill put on the <a href="/wiki/Special_Relationship" title="Special Relationship">special relationship</a> with the United States and disappointed by the American treatment of its British allies.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1942, Eden was given the additional role of <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a>. He was considered for various other major jobs during and after the war, including Commander-in-Chief Middle East in 1942 (which would have been a very unusual appointment as Eden was a civilian; General <a href="/wiki/Harold_Alexander,_1st_Earl_Alexander_of_Tunis" title="Harold Alexander, 1st Earl Alexander of Tunis">Harold Alexander</a> would be appointed), <a href="/wiki/Governor-General_of_India" title="Governor-General of India">Viceroy of India</a> in 1943 (General <a href="/wiki/Archibald_Wavell,_1st_Earl_Wavell" title="Archibald Wavell, 1st Earl Wavell">Archibald Wavell</a> was appointed to this job) or <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">Secretary-General</a> of the newly formed <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations Organisation</a> in 1945.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In 1943, with the revelation of the <a href="/wiki/Katyn_massacre" title="Katyn massacre">Katyn massacre</a>, Eden refused to help the <a href="/wiki/Polish_government-in-exile" title="Polish government-in-exile">Polish Government in Exile</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Andrews1976_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Andrews1976-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Eden supported the idea of post-war <a href="/wiki/Expulsion_of_Germans_from_Czechoslovakia" title="Expulsion of Germans from Czechoslovakia">expulsion of ethnic Germans from Czechoslovakia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early 1943, Eden blocked a request from the Bulgarian authorities to aid with deporting part of the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Bulgaria" title="History of the Jews in Bulgaria">Jewish population</a> from newly acquired Bulgarian territories to the British territory of Mandatory Palestine. After his refusal, some of the people were transported to <a href="/wiki/Treblinka_extermination_camp" title="Treblinka extermination camp">Treblinka extermination camp</a> in <a href="/wiki/Occupation_of_Poland_(1939%E2%80%931945)" title="Occupation of Poland (1939–1945)">Nazi-occupied Poland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1944, Eden went to Moscow to negotiate with the Soviet Union at the <a href="/wiki/Moscow_Conference_(1944)" title="Moscow Conference (1944)">Tolstoy Conference</a>. Eden also opposed the <a href="/wiki/Morgenthau_Plan" title="Morgenthau Plan">Morgenthau Plan</a> to deindustrialise Germany. After the <a href="/wiki/Stalag_Luft_III_murders" title="Stalag Luft III murders">Stalag Luft III murders</a>, he vowed in the House of Commons to bring the perpetrators of the crime to "exemplary justice", which led to a successful manhunt after the war by the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">Royal Air Force</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Special_Investigation_Branch" title="Special Investigation Branch">Special Investigation Branch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Andrews1976_104-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Andrews1976-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> During the <a href="/wiki/Yalta_Conference" title="Yalta Conference">Yalta Conference</a> (February 1945) he pressed the Soviet Union and the United States to allow France a <a href="/wiki/French_occupation_zone_in_Germany" title="French occupation zone in Germany">zone of occupation</a> in <a href="/wiki/Allied-occupied_Germany" title="Allied-occupied Germany">post-war Germany</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden's eldest son, <a href="/wiki/Pilot_officer" title="Pilot officer">Pilot officer</a> Simon Gascoigne Eden, went <a href="/wiki/Missing_in_action" title="Missing in action">missing in action</a> and was later declared dead; he was serving as a <a href="/wiki/Navigator" title="Navigator">navigator</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">Royal Air Force</a> in <a href="/wiki/Myanmar" title="Myanmar">Burma</a> in June 1945.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> There was a close bond between Eden and Simon, and Simon's death was a great personal shock to his father. Mrs Eden reportedly reacted to the loss of her son differently, which led to a breakdown in the marriage. De Gaulle wrote him a personal letter of <a href="/wiki/Condolence" class="mw-redirect" title="Condolence">condolence</a> in French.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1945, he was mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Halvdan_Koht" title="Halvdan Koht">Halvdan Koht</a> among seven candidates who were qualified for the <a href="/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize" title="Nobel Peace Prize">Nobel Peace Prize</a>. However, he did not explicitly nominate any of them. The person who was actually nominated was <a href="/wiki/Cordell_Hull" title="Cordell Hull">Cordell Hull</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Postwar.2C_1945.E2.80.931955"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Postwar,_1945–1955">Postwar, 1945–1955</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Postwar, 1945–1955">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="In_opposition.2C_1945.E2.80.931951"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_opposition,_1945–1951">In opposition, 1945–1951</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: In opposition, 1945–1951">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a> won the 1945 election, Eden went into opposition as <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy Leader of the Conservative Party (UK)">Deputy Leader of the Conservative Party</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Many felt that Churchill should have retired and allowed Eden to become party leader, but Churchill refused to consider the idea. As early as the spring of 1946, Eden openly asked Churchill to retire in his favour.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> He was in any case depressed by the end of his first marriage and the death of his eldest son.
