Churchill was, in many ways, only "part-time Leader of the Opposition"<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> because of his many journeys abroad and his literary work, and left the day-to-day work largely to Eden, who was largely regarded as lacking a sense of party politics and contact with the common man.<sup id="cite_ref-Time-man_who_waited_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Time-man_who_waited-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> In the opposition years, however, he developed some knowledge about domestic affairs and created the idea of a "property-owning-democracy", which <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a>'s government attempted to achieve decades later. His domestic agenda is overall considered to be <a href="/wiki/Centre-left_politics" title="Centre-left politics">centre-left</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Return_to_government.2C_1951.E2.80.931955"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_government,_1951–1955">Return to government, 1951–1955</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Return to government, 1951–1955">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3><p>
In 1951 the Conservatives returned to office and Eden became Foreign Secretary for a third time.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> Churchill was largely a figurehead in the government, and Eden had effective control of British foreign policy for the second time, with the decline of the empire and the intensifying of the <a href="/wiki/Cold_War" title="Cold War">Cold War</a>. Churchill wanted to appoint Eden <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Deputy Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Deputy Prime Minister</a> as well as Foreign Secretary, but the <a href="/wiki/George_VI" title="George VI">King</a> objected and said that the office did not exist in the UK constitution and might interfere with his ability to appoint a successor.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Thus, Eden was not appointed Deputy Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> However, he still considered himself Churchill's "second-in-command" and had been regarded as Churchill's "crown prince" since 1942.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001,_Paris,_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg/220px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="151" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg/330px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg/440px-Bundesarchiv_Bild_183-27106-0001%2C_Paris%2C_Verhandlungen_%C3%BCber_NATO-Beitritt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="549" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/London_and_Paris_Conferences" title="London and Paris Conferences">Negotiations in London and Paris</a> in 1954 ended the <a href="/wiki/Allied-occupied_Germany" title="Allied-occupied Germany">allied occupation</a> of <a href="/wiki/West_Germany" title="West Germany">West Germany</a> and allowed for its rearmament as a NATO member.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Eden's biographer Richard Lamb said that Eden bullied Churchill into going back on commitments to <a href="/wiki/European_integration" title="European integration">European unity</a> made in opposition. The truth appears to be more complex. Britain was still a world power or at least trying to be one in 1945–55, with the concept of sovereignty not as discredited as on the Continent. The United States encouraged moves towards European federalism so that it could withdraw troops and have the <a href="/wiki/West_German_rearmament" title="West German rearmament">Germans rearmed under supervision</a>. Eden was less <a href="/wiki/Atlanticism" title="Atlanticism">Atlanticist</a> than Churchill and had little time for European federalism. He wanted firm alliances with France and other Western European powers to contain Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Half of British trade was then with the <a href="/wiki/Sterling_area" title="Sterling area">sterling area</a> and only a quarter with Western Europe. Despite later talk of "lost opportunities", even Macmillan, who had been an active member of the <a href="/wiki/European_Movement" class="mw-redirect" title="European Movement">European Movement</a> after the war, acknowledged in February 1952 that <a href="/wiki/Special_Relationship" title="Special Relationship">Britain's special relationship with the United States</a> and the Commonwealth would prevent it from joining a federal Europe at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Charmley_1995,_p299_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charmley_1995,_p299-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Eden was also irritated by Churchill's hankering for a summit meeting with the Soviet Union in 1953 after <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Joseph_Stalin" title="Death and state funeral of Joseph Stalin">Stalin's death</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Charmley_1995,_p299_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charmley_1995,_p299-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> Eden became seriously ill from a series of botched bile duct operations in April 1953 that nearly killed him. After that, he had frequent bouts of poor physical health and <a href="/wiki/Depression_(mood)" title="Depression (mood)">psychological depression</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the ending of the <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British Raj</a> in India, British interest in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_East" title="Middle East">Middle East</a> remained strong. Britain had treaty relations with <a href="/wiki/Jordan" title="Jordan">Jordan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Iraq" title="Kingdom of Iraq">Iraq</a> and was the protecting power for <a href="/wiki/Sheikhdom_of_Kuwait" title="Sheikhdom of Kuwait">Kuwait</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Trucial_States" title="Trucial States">Trucial States</a>, the colonial power in <a href="/wiki/Aden_Colony" title="Aden Colony">Aden</a>, and the occupying power in the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>. Many right-wing Conservative MPs, organised in the so-called <i>Suez Group</i>, sought to retain the imperial role, but economic pressures made maintenance of it increasingly difficult. Britain sought to maintain its huge military base in the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal zone</a> and, in the face of Egyptian resentment, to further develop its alliance with Iraq, and the hope was that the Americans would assist Britain, possibly by finance. While the Americans co-operated with the British in the <a href="/wiki/28_Mordad_coup" class="mw-redirect" title="28 Mordad coup">28 Mordad coup</a> against the <a href="/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh" title="Mohammad Mosaddegh">Mosaddegh</a> government in <a href="/wiki/Iran" title="Iran">Iran</a> after it had nationalised British oil interests, the Americans developed their own relations in the region and took a positive view of the Egyptian <a href="/wiki/Free_Officers_Movement_(Egypt)" title="Free Officers Movement (Egypt)">Free Officers</a> and developed <a href="/wiki/Saudi_Arabia%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Saudi Arabia–United States relations">friendly relations</a> with <a href="/wiki/Saudi_Arabia" title="Saudi Arabia">Saudi Arabia</a>. Britain was eventually forced to withdraw from the canal zone, and the <a href="/wiki/Baghdad_Pact" class="mw-redirect" title="Baghdad Pact">Baghdad Pact</a> security treaty was not supported by the United States, which left Eden vulnerable to the charge of having failed to maintain British prestige.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1stIndochinaWar003.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/1stIndochinaWar003.jpg/220px-1stIndochinaWar003.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/1stIndochinaWar003.jpg/330px-1stIndochinaWar003.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/1stIndochinaWar003.jpg/440px-1stIndochinaWar003.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="953" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Geneva_Conference_(1954)" class="mw-redirect" title="Geneva Conference (1954)">Geneva Conference</a>, 21 July 1954. Last plenary session on <a href="/wiki/Indochina" class="mw-redirect" title="Indochina">Indochina</a> in the Palais des Nations.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Eden had grave misgivings about American foreign policy under Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles" title="John Foster Dulles">John Foster Dulles</a> and President <a href="/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower" title="Dwight D. Eisenhower">Dwight D. Eisenhower</a>. As early as March 1953, Eisenhower was concerned at the escalating costs of defence and the increase of state power that it would bring.<sup id="cite_ref-Charmley_1995,_pp_274–5_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charmley_1995,_pp_274–5-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Eden was irked by Dulles's policy of "<a href="/wiki/Brinkmanship" title="Brinkmanship">brinkmanship</a>", the display of muscle, in relations with the <a href="/wiki/Second_World" title="Second World">communist world</a>. In particular, both had heated exchanges with one another regarding the proposed American aerial strike operation (<a href="/wiki/Operation_Vulture" title="Operation Vulture"><i>Vulture</i></a>) to try to save the beleaguered <a href="/wiki/French_Union" title="French Union">French Union</a> garrison at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Dien_Bien_Phu" title="Battle of Dien Bien Phu">Battle of Dien Bien Phu</a> in early 1954.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The operation was cancelled, in part, because of Eden's refusal to commit to it for fear of Chinese intervention and ultimately a <a href="/wiki/World_War_III" title="World War III">third world war</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Dulles then walked out early in the <a href="/wiki/Geneva_Conference_(1954)" class="mw-redirect" title="Geneva Conference (1954)">Geneva Conference</a> talks and was critical of the American decision not to sign it. Nevertheless, the success of the conference ranked as the outstanding achievement of Eden's third term in the <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Office" class="mw-redirect" title="Foreign Office">Foreign Office</a>. During the summer and autumn of 1954, the Anglo-Egyptian agreement to withdraw all British forces from Egypt was also negotiated and ratified.
</p><p>There were concerns that if the <a href="/wiki/European_Defence_Community" class="mw-redirect" title="European Defence Community">European Defence Community</a> was not ratified as it wanted, the United States might withdraw into defending only the <a href="/wiki/Western_Hemisphere" title="Western Hemisphere">Western Hemisphere</a>, but recent documentary evidence confirms that the US intended to withdraw troops from Europe anyway even if the EDC was ratified.<sup id="cite_ref-Charmley_1995,_pp_274–5_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charmley_1995,_pp_274–5-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> After the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_(France)" title="National Assembly (France)">French National Assembly</a> rejected the EDC in August 1954, Eden tried to come up with a viable alternative. Between 11 and 17 September, he visited every major West European capital to negotiate <a href="/wiki/West_Germany" title="West Germany">West Germany</a> becoming a sovereign state and entering the <a href="/wiki/Western_European_Union" title="Western European Union">Western European Union</a> prior to it entering <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">NATO</a>. <a href="/wiki/Paul-Henri_Spaak" title="Paul-Henri Spaak">Paul-Henri Spaak</a> said that Eden "saved the Atlantic alliance".<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1954, he was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Order of the Garter</a><sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> and became <i>Sir Anthony Eden</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_1955.E2.80.931957"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_1955–1957">Prime Minister, 1955–1957<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, 1955–1957">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Eden_ministry" title="Eden ministry">Eden ministry</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox" style="padding-bottom:2px; padding-top:2px;"><tbody><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image" style="border-bottom:0; padding-bottom:1px;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Eden,_Anthony.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="Anthony Eden"><img alt="Anthony Eden" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg/220px-Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="260" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg/330px-Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg/440px-Eden%2C_Anthony.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3772" data-file-height="4458" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="padding-top:2px;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b><span style="font-size:120%">Premiership of Anthony Eden</span></b></span><br />6 April 1955&#160;–&#32;9 January 1957</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Monarch</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Cabinet</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Eden_ministry" title="Eden ministry">Eden ministry</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Election</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/1955_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1955 United Kingdom general election">1955</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><a href="/wiki/Seat_of_government" title="Seat of government">Seat</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/10_Downing_Street" title="10 Downing Street">10 Downing Street</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><div style="line-height:normal; padding-top:1px;"><div style="width:100%"><div style="float: left; text-align:left;padding-right:0.5em;" class="noprint">&#8592;&#160;<a href="/wiki/Second_premiership_of_Winston_Churchill" class="mw-redirect" title="Second premiership of Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a></div><div style="float: right; text-align:right;padding-left:0.5em;" class="noprint"><a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Harold_Macmillan" class="mw-redirect" title="Premiership of Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a>&#160;&#8594;</div></div></div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">
                                                   <hr class="nomobile" style="background:#eee; height:5px; clear:both; margin:0 0 8px;" /> <div class="center"><div style="display:inline-block; padding:6px 0 2px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_(HM_Government,_1952-2022).svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/100px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="100" height="82" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/150px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg/200px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%2C_1952-2022%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1530" data-file-height="1256" /></a></span></div><br /><div style="line-height:normal; min-height:1px; padding-bottom:2px;"><a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_HM_Government" class="mw-redirect" title="Coat of arms of HM Government">Coat of arms of HM Government</a></div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In April 1955 Churchill retired, and Eden succeeded him as prime minister. He was a very popular figure as a result of his long wartime service and his famous good looks and charm. His famous words "Peace comes first, always" added to his already substantial popularity.
</p><p>On taking office he immediately called a <a href="/wiki/1955_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1955 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> for 26 May 1955, at which he increased the Conservative majority from seventeen to sixty, an increase in majority that broke a ninety-year record for any UK government. The 1955 general election was the last in which the Conservatives won the majority share of the votes in Scotland. However, Eden had never held a domestic portfolio and had little experience in economic matters. He left these areas to his lieutenants such as <a href="/wiki/Rab_Butler" title="Rab Butler">Rab Butler</a>, and concentrated largely on foreign policy, forming a close relationship with US President <a href="/wiki/Dwight_Eisenhower" class="mw-redirect" title="Dwight Eisenhower">Dwight Eisenhower</a>. Eden's attempts to maintain overall control of the Foreign Office drew widespread criticism.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Avoid_weasel_words" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:Avoid weasel words"><span title="The material near this tag may use weasel words or too-vague attribution. (November 2021)">from whom?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Eden has the distinction of being the British prime minister to oversee the lowest <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Unemployment in the United Kingdom">unemployment</a> figures of the <a href="/wiki/Postwar_Britain_(1945%E2%80%931979)" class="mw-redirect" title="Postwar Britain (1945–1979)">post-World War II era</a>, with unemployment standing at just over 215,000—barely one per cent of the workforce—in July 1955.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Suez_.281956.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Suez_(1956)">Suez (1956)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Suez (1956)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Suez_Crisis" title="Suez Crisis">Suez Crisis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Operation_Musketeer_(1956)" title="Operation Musketeer (1956)">Operation Musketeer (1956)</a></div>
<p>The alliance with the US proved not universal, however, when in July 1956 <a href="/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser" title="Gamal Abdel Nasser">Gamal Abdel Nasser</a>, President of <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>, nationalised the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a>, following the withdrawal of Anglo-American funding for the <a href="/wiki/Aswan_Dam" title="Aswan Dam">Aswan Dam</a>. Eden believed the nationalisation was in violation of the Anglo-Egyptian treaty of 1954 that Nasser had signed with the British and French governments on 19 October 1954. This view was shared by Labour leader <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Gaitskell" title="Hugh Gaitskell">Hugh Gaitskell</a> and Liberal leader <a href="/wiki/Jo_Grimond" title="Jo Grimond">Jo Grimond</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> In 1956 the Suez Canal was of vital importance since over two-thirds of the oil supplies of Western Europe (60 million tons annually) passed through it on 15,000 ships a year, one-third of them British; three-quarters of all Canal shipping belonged to NATO countries. Britain's total oil reserve at the time of the nationalisation was enough for only six weeks.<sup id="cite_ref-historytoday1_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-historytoday1-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The Soviet Union was certain to veto any sanctions against Nasser at the United Nations. Britain and a conference of other nations met in London following the nationalisation in an attempt to resolve the crisis through diplomatic means. However, the Eighteen Nations Proposals, including an offer of Egyptian representation on the board of the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="Suez Canal Company">Suez Canal Company</a> and a share of profits, were rejected by Nasser.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Eden feared that Nasser intended to form an Arab Alliance that would threaten to cut off oil supplies to Europe and, in conjunction with France, decided he should be removed from power.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most people believed that Nasser was acting from legitimate patriotic concerns and the nationalisation was determined by the Foreign Office to be deliberately provocative but not illegal. The Attorney General, Sir <a href="/wiki/Reginald_Manningham-Buller" class="mw-redirect" title="Reginald Manningham-Buller">Reginald Manningham-Buller</a>, was not asked for his opinion officially but made his view, that the government's contemplated armed strike against Egypt would be unlawful, known through the Lord Chancellor.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Anthony_Nutting" title="Anthony Nutting">Anthony Nutting</a> recalled that Eden told him, "What's all this nonsense about isolating Nasser or 'neutralising' him as you call it? I want him destroyed, can't you understand? I want him murdered, and if you and the Foreign Office don't agree, then you'd better come to the cabinet and explain why." When Nutting pointed out that they had no alternative government to replace Nasser, Eden apparently replied, "I don't give a damn if there's anarchy and chaos in Egypt."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> At a private meeting at Downing Street on 16 October 1956 Eden showed several ministers a plan, submitted two days earlier by France. Israel would invade Egypt, Britain and France would give an ultimatum telling both sides to stop and, when one refused, send in forces to enforce the ultimatum, separate the two sides – and occupy the Canal and get rid of Nasser. When Nutting suggested the Americans should be consulted Eden replied, "I will not bring the Americans into this ... Dulles has done enough damage as it is. This has nothing to do with the Americans. We and the French must decide what to do and we alone."<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Eden openly admitted his view of the crisis was shaped by his experiences in the two world wars, writing, "We are all marked to some extent by the stamp of our generation, mine is that of the <a href="/wiki/Assassination_in_Sarajevo" class="mw-redirect" title="Assassination in Sarajevo">assassination in Sarajevo</a> and all that flowed from it. It is impossible to read the record now and not feel that we had a responsibility for always being a lap behind ... Always a lap behind, a fatal lap."<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was no question of the pathway to an immediate military response to the crisis – <a href="/wiki/British_Cyprus" title="British Cyprus">Cyprus</a> had no deep-water harbours, which meant that <a href="/wiki/Crown_Colony_of_Malta" title="Crown Colony of Malta">Malta</a>, several days' sailing from Egypt, would have to be the main concentration point for an invasion fleet if the Libyan government would not permit a land invasion from its territory.<sup id="cite_ref-historytoday1_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-historytoday1-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> Eden initially considered using British forces in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Libya" title="Kingdom of Libya">Kingdom of Libya</a> to regain the Canal, but then decided this risked inflaming Arab opinion.<sup id="cite_ref-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Unlike the French prime minister <a href="/wiki/Guy_Mollet" title="Guy Mollet">Guy Mollet</a>, who saw regaining the Canal as the primary objective, Eden believed the real need was to remove Nasser from office. He hoped that if the Egyptian army was swiftly and humiliatingly defeated by the Anglo-French forces the Egyptian people would rise up against Nasser. Eden told Field Marshal <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Montgomery" title="Bernard Montgomery">Bernard Montgomery</a> that the overall aim of the mission was simply, "To knock Nasser off his perch."<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> In the absence of a popular uprising Eden and Mollet would say that Egyptian forces were incapable of defending their country and therefore Anglo-French forces would have to return to guard the Suez Canal.
</p><p>Eden believed that if Nasser were seen to get away with seizing the Canal then Egypt and other Arab countries might move closer to the Soviet Union. At that time, the Middle East accounted for 80–90 percent of Western Europe's oil supply. Other Middle East countries might also be encouraged to nationalise their oil industries. The invasion, he contended at the time, and again in a 1967 interview, was aimed at maintaining the sanctity of international agreements and at preventing future unilateral denunciation of treaties.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_96-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Eden was energetic during the crisis in using the media, including the <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a>, to incite public opinion to support his views of the need to overthrow Nasser.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> In September 1956 a plan was drawn up to reduce the flow of water in the <a href="/wiki/Nile" title="Nile">Nile</a> by using dams in an attempt to damage Nasser's position. However, the plan was abandoned because it would take months to implement, and due to fears that it could affect other countries such as <a href="/wiki/Uganda_Protectorate" class="mw-redirect" title="Uganda Protectorate">Uganda</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kenya_Colony" title="Kenya Colony">Kenya</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 25 September 1956, the Chancellor of the Exchequer <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> met informally with President Eisenhower at the White House; he misread Eisenhower's determination to avoid war and told Eden that the Americans would not in any way oppose the attempt to topple Nasser.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Though Eden had known Eisenhower for years and had many direct contacts during the crisis, he also misread the situation. The Americans saw themselves as the champion of <a href="/wiki/Decolonization" title="Decolonization">decolonisation</a> and refused to support any move that could be seen as imperialism or colonialism. Eisenhower felt the crisis had to be handled peacefully; he told Eden that American public opinion would not support a military solution. Eden and other leading British officials incorrectly believed Nasser's support for Palestinian militia against Israel, as well as his attempts to destabilise pro-western regimes in Iraq and other Arab states, would deter the US from intervening with the operation. Eisenhower specifically warned that the Americans, and the world, "would be outraged" unless all peaceful routes had been exhausted, and even then "the eventual price might become far too heavy".<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> At the root of the problem was the fact that Eden felt that Britain was still an independent world power. His lack of sympathy for British integration into Europe, manifested in his <a href="/wiki/Euroscepticism_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Euroscepticism in the United Kingdom">scepticism</a> about the fledgling <a href="/wiki/European_Economic_Community" title="European Economic Community">European Economic Community</a> (EEC), was another aspect of his belief in Britain's independent role in world affairs.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Israel invaded the <a href="/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula" title="Sinai Peninsula">Sinai peninsula</a> at the end of October 1956. Britain and France moved in ostensibly to separate the two sides and bring peace, but in fact to regain control of the canal and overthrow Nasser. The United States immediately and strongly opposed the invasion. The United Nations denounced the invasion, the Soviets were bellicose, and only New Zealand, Australia, West Germany and South Africa spoke out for Britain's position.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Suez Canal was of lesser economic importance to the US, which acquired only 15 percent of its oil through that route (compared to well over half of the total oil supply to the UK) at the time. Eisenhower wanted to broker international peace in "fragile" regions. He did not see Nasser as a serious threat to the West, but he was concerned that the Soviets, who were well known to want a permanent warm water base for their <a href="/wiki/Black_Sea_Fleet" title="Black Sea Fleet">Black Sea Fleet</a> in the Mediterranean, might side with Egypt. Eisenhower feared a pro-Soviet backlash amongst the Arab nations if, as seemed likely, Egypt suffered an humiliating defeat at the hands of the British, French and Israelis.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden, who faced domestic pressure from his party to take action, as well as stopping the decline of British influence in the Middle East,<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> had ignored Britain's financial dependence on the US in the wake of the Second World War, and had assumed the US would automatically endorse whatever action taken by its closest ally. At the 'Law not War' rally in <a href="/wiki/Trafalgar_Square" title="Trafalgar Square">Trafalgar Square</a> on 4 November 1956, Eden was ridiculed by <a href="/wiki/Aneurin_Bevan" title="Aneurin Bevan">Aneurin Bevan</a>: "Sir Anthony Eden has been pretending that he is now invading Egypt to strengthen the United Nations. Every burglar of course could say the same thing; he could argue that he was entering the house to train the police. So, if Sir Anthony Eden is sincere in what he is saying, and he may be, then he is too <i>stupid</i> to be a prime minister". Public opinion was mixed; some historians think that the majority of public opinion in the UK was on Eden's side.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden was forced to bow to American diplomatic and financial pressure, and protests at home, by calling a ceasefire when Anglo-French forces had captured only 23 of the 120 miles of the canal. With the US threatening to withdraw its financial support for the <a href="/wiki/Pound_sterling" title="Pound sterling">pound sterling</a>, the cabinet divided and the Chancellor of the Exchequer <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> threatening to resign unless an immediate ceasefire was called, Eden was under immense pressure. He considered defying the calls until the commander on the ground told him it could take up to six days for the Anglo-French troops to secure the entire Canal zone. Therefore, a ceasefire was called at quarter past midnight on 7 November.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In his 1987 book <i><a href="/wiki/Spycatcher" title="Spycatcher">Spycatcher</a></i> <a href="/wiki/Peter_Wright_(MI5_officer)" title="Peter Wright (MI5 officer)">Peter Wright</a> said that, following the imposed ending to the military operation, Eden reactivated the assassination option for a second time. By this time virtually all MI6 agents in Egypt had been rounded up by Nasser, and a new operation, using renegade Egyptian officers, was drawn up. It failed principally because the cache of weapons which had been hidden on the outskirts of Cairo was found to be defective.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Suez badly damaged Eden's reputation for statesmanship, and led to a breakdown in his health. He went on vacation to <a href="/wiki/Jamaica" title="Jamaica">Jamaica</a> in November 1956, at a time when he was still determined to soldier on as prime minister. His health, however, did not improve, and during his absence from London his Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Harold_Macmillan" title="Harold Macmillan">Harold Macmillan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rab_Butler" title="Rab Butler">Rab Butler</a> worked to manoeuvre him out of office. On the morning of the ceasefire Eisenhower agreed to meet with Eden to publicly resolve their differences, but this offer was later withdrawn after Secretary of State Dulles advised that it could inflame the Middle Eastern situation further.<sup id="cite_ref-Kyle,_Keith_p._489_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kyle,_Keith_p._489-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><i><a href="/wiki/The_Observer" title="The Observer">The Observer</a></i> newspaper accused Eden of lying to Parliament over the Suez Crisis, while MPs from all parties criticised his calling a ceasefire before the Canal was taken. Churchill, while publicly supportive of Eden's actions, privately criticised his successor for not seeing the military operation through to its conclusion. Eden easily survived a vote of confidence in the House of Commons on 8 November.<sup id="cite_ref-Kyle,_Keith_p._489_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kyle,_Keith_p._489-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1957_resignation">1957 resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: 1957 resignation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>While Eden was on holiday in <a href="/wiki/Goldeneye_(estate)" title="Goldeneye (estate)">Goldeneye Estate</a> in <a href="/wiki/Oracabessa" title="Oracabessa">Oracabessa Bay</a> in <a href="/wiki/Colony_of_Jamaica" title="Colony of Jamaica">Jamaica</a>, other members of the government discussed on 20 November 1956 how to counter charges that the UK and France had worked in collusion with Israel to seize the Canal, but decided there was very little evidence in the public domain.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On his return from Jamaica on 14 December, Eden still hoped to continue as prime minister. He had lost his traditional base of support on the Tory left and amongst moderate opinion nationally, but appears to have hoped to rebuild a new base of support amongst the Tory right.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> However, his political position had eroded during his absence. He wished to make a statement attacking Nasser as a puppet of the Soviets, attacking the United Nations and speaking of the "lessons of the 1930s", but was prevented from doing so by Macmillan, Butler and <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_5th_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 5th Marquess of Salisbury">Lord Salisbury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On his return to the House of Commons (17 December), he slipped into the Chamber largely unacknowledged by his own party. One Conservative MP rose to wave his <a href="/wiki/Order_Paper" title="Order Paper">Order Paper</a>, only to have to sit down in embarrassment whilst Labour MPs laughed.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> On 18 December he addressed the <a href="/wiki/1922_committee" class="mw-redirect" title="1922 committee">1922 committee</a> (Conservative backbenchers), declaring "as long as I live, I shall never apologise for what we did", but was unable to answer a question about the validity of the <a href="/wiki/Tripartite_Declaration_of_1950" title="Tripartite Declaration of 1950">Tripartite Declaration of 1950</a> (which he had in fact reaffirmed in April 1955, two days before becoming Prime Minister).<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> In his final statement to the House of Commons as prime minister (20 December 1956), he performed well in a difficult debate, but told MPs that "there was not foreknowledge that Israel would attack Egypt". Victor Rothwell writes that the knowledge of his having misled the House of Commons in this way must have hung over him thereafter, as was the concern that the US Administration might demand that Britain pay reparations to Egypt.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247_153-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Papers released in January 1987 showed the entire cabinet had been informed of the plan on 23 October 1956.<sup id="cite_ref-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org_139-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden suffered another fever at <a href="/wiki/Chequers" title="Chequers">Chequers</a> over Christmas, but was still talking of going on an official trip to the USSR in April 1957, wanting a full inquiry into the <a href="/wiki/Crabb_affair" class="mw-redirect" title="Crabb affair">Crabb affair</a> and badgering <a href="/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone" title="Quintin Hogg, Baron Hailsham of St Marylebone">Lord Hailsham</a> (<a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Admiralty" title="First Lord of the Admiralty">First Lord of the Admiralty</a>) about the £6m being spent on oil storage at Malta.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247_153-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p244,_247-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden resigned on 9 January 1957, after his doctors warned him his life was at stake if he continued in office.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Charmley" title="John Charmley">John Charmley</a> writes "Ill-health ... provide(d) a dignified reason for an action (i.e. resignation) which would, in any event, have been necessary."<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Rothwell writes that "mystery persists" over exactly how Eden was persuaded to resign, although the limited evidence suggests that Butler, who was expected to succeed him as prime minister, was at the centre of the intrigue. Rothwell writes that Eden's fevers were "nasty but brief and not life-threatening" and that there may have been "manipulation of medical evidence" to make Eden's health seem "even worse" than it was. Macmillan wrote in his diary that "nature had provided a real health reason" when a "diplomatic illness" might otherwise have had to be invented. David Carlton (1981) even suggested that the Palace might have been involved, a suggestion discussed by Rothwell. As early as spring 1954 Eden had been indifferent to cultivating good relations with the new Queen. Eden is known to have favoured a Japanese or <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavian</a> style monarchy (i.e. with no involvement in politics whatsoever) and in January 1956 he had insisted that <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev" title="Nikita Khrushchev">Nikita Khrushchev</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Bulganin" title="Nikolai Bulganin">Nikolai Bulganin</a> spend only the minimum amount of time in talks with the Queen. Evidence also exists that the Palace was concerned at not being kept fully informed during the Suez Crisis. In the 1960s, Clarissa Eden was observed to speak of the Queen "in an extremely hostile and belittling way", and in an interview in 1976, Eden commented that he "would not claim she was pro-Suez".<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although the media expected Butler would get the nod as Eden's successor, a survey of the cabinet taken for the Queen showed Macmillan was the nearly unanimous choice, and he became prime minister on 10 January 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Shortly afterwards Eden and his wife left England for a holiday in New Zealand.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Suez_in_retrospect">Suez in retrospect</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Suez in retrospect">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p><a href="/wiki/A._J._P._Taylor" title="A. J. P. Taylor">A. J. P. Taylor</a> wrote in the 1970s: "Eden … destroyed (his reputation as a peacemaker) and led Great Britain to one of the greatest humiliations in her history … (he) seemed to take on a new personality. He acted impatiently and on impulse. Previously flexible he now relied on dogma, denouncing Nasser as a second Hitler. Though he claimed to be upholding international law, he in fact disregarded the United Nations Organisation which he had helped to create...The outcome was pathetic rather than tragic".<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Biographer D. R. Thorpe says Eden's four goals were to secure the canal; to make sure it remained open and that oil shipments would continue; to depose Nasser; and to prevent the USSR from gaining influence. "The immediate consequence of the crisis was that the Suez Canal was blocked, oil supplies were interrupted, Nasser's position as the leader of Arab nationalism was strengthened, and the way was left open for Russian intrusion into the Middle East.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Michael_Foot" title="Michael Foot">Michael Foot</a> pushed for a special inquiry along the lines of the Parliamentary Inquiry into the <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallipoli Campaign">Attack on the Dardanelles</a> in the First World War, although <a href="/wiki/Harold_Wilson" title="Harold Wilson">Harold Wilson</a> (Labour Prime Minister 1964–70 and 1974–76) regarded the matter as a can of worms best left unopened. This talk ceased after the defeat of the Arab armies by Israel in the <a href="/wiki/Six-Day_War" title="Six-Day War">Six-Day War</a> of 1967, after which Eden received a lot of fanmail telling him that he had been right, and his reputation, not least in Israel and the United States, soared.<sup id="cite_ref-historytoday1_132-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-historytoday1-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> In 1986 Eden's official biographer <a href="/wiki/Robert_Rhodes_James" title="Robert Rhodes James">Robert Rhodes James</a> re-evaluated sympathetically Eden's stance over Suez<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> and in 1990, following the <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait" class="mw-redirect" title="Invasion of Kuwait">Iraqi invasion of Kuwait</a>, James asked: "Who can now claim that Eden was wrong?".<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Such arguments turn mostly on whether, as a matter of policy, the Suez operation was fundamentally flawed or whether, as such "revisionists" thought, the lack of American support conveyed the impression that the West was divided and weak. <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Nutting" title="Anthony Nutting">Anthony Nutting</a>, who resigned as a Foreign Office Minister over Suez, expressed the former view in 1967, the year of the <a href="/wiki/Six-Day_War" title="Six-Day War">Arab–Israeli Six-Day War</a>, when he wrote that "we had sown the wind of bitterness and we were to reap the whirlwind of revenge and rebellion".<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> Conversely, Jonathan Pearson argues in <i>Sir Anthony Eden and the Suez Crisis: Reluctant Gamble</i> (2002) that Eden was more reluctant and less bellicose than most historians have judged. <a href="/wiki/D._R._Thorpe" title="D. R. Thorpe">D. R. Thorpe</a>, another of Eden's biographers, writes that Suez was "a truly tragic end to his premiership, and one that came to assume a disproportionate importance in any assessment of his careers"; he suggests that had the Suez venture succeeded, "there would almost certainly have been no Middle East war in 1967, and probably no <a href="/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War" title="Yom Kippur War">Yom Kippur War</a> in 1973 also".<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Guy_Millard" title="Guy Millard">Guy Millard</a>, one of Eden's Private Secretaries, who thirty years later, in a radio interview, spoke publicly for the first time on the crisis, made an insider's judgement about Eden: "It was his mistake of course and a tragic and disastrous mistake for him. I think he overestimated the importance of Nasser, Egypt, the Canal, even of the Middle East."<sup id="cite_ref-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org_139-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-qjmed.oxfordjournals.org-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> While British actions in 1956 have usually been described as "imperialistic", the main motivation was economic. Eden was a liberal supporter of nationalist ambitions, including over Sudanese independence, and his 1954 Suez Canal Base Agreement, which withdrew British troops from Suez in return for certain guarantees, was negotiated with the Conservative Party against Churchill's wishes.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rothwell believes that Eden should have cancelled the Suez Invasion plans in mid-October, when the Anglo-French negotiations at the United Nations were making some headway, and that in 1956 the Arab countries threw away a chance to make peace with Israel on her existing borders.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Britain.E2.80.93France_rejected_plan_for_union"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Britain–France_rejected_plan_for_union">Britain–France rejected plan for union</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Britain–France rejected plan for union">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>British Government cabinet papers from September 1956, during Eden's term as prime minister, have shown that French Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Guy_Mollet" title="Guy Mollet">Guy Mollet</a> approached the British Government suggesting the idea of an <a href="/wiki/Franco-British_Union#Suez_Crisis_(1956)" title="Franco-British Union">economic and political union between France and Great Britain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> This was a similar offer, in reverse, to that made by Churchill (drawing on a plan devised by <a href="/wiki/Leo_Amery" title="Leo Amery">Leo Amery</a><sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>) in June 1940.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The offer by <a href="/wiki/Guy_Mollet" title="Guy Mollet">Guy Mollet</a> was referred to by Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Colville_(civil_servant)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Colville (civil servant)">John Colville</a>, Churchill's former private secretary, in his collected diaries, <i>The Fringes of Power</i> (1985), his having gleaned the information in 1957 from Air Chief Marshal Sir <a href="/wiki/William_Dickson_(RAF_officer)" title="William Dickson (RAF officer)">William Dickson</a> during an air flight (and, according to Colville, after several whiskies and soda).<sup id="cite_ref-Postscript_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Postscript-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Mollet's request for Union with Britain was rejected by Eden, but the additional possibility of France joining the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a> was considered, although similarly rejected. Colville noted, in respect of Suez, that Eden and his Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/Selwyn_Lloyd" title="Selwyn Lloyd">Selwyn Lloyd</a> "felt still more beholden to the French on account of this offer".<sup id="cite_ref-Postscript_173-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Postscript-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Eden also resigned from the House of Commons when he stood down as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_608–9_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_608–9-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Eden kept in touch with Lord Salisbury, agreeing with him that Macmillan had been the better choice as prime minister, but sympathising with his resignation over Macmillan's Cyprus policy. Despite a series of letters in which Macmillan almost begged him for a personal endorsement prior to the <a href="/wiki/1959_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1959 United Kingdom general election">1959 election</a>, Eden only issued a declaration of support for the Conservative Government.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_609–10_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_609–10-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Eden retained much of his personal popularity in Britain and contemplated returning to Parliament. Several Conservative MPs were reportedly willing to give up their seats for him, although the party hierarchy was less keen. He finally gave up such hopes in late 1960 after an exhausting speaking tour of Yorkshire.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_608–9_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_608–9-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Macmillan initially offered to recommend him for a viscountcy, which Eden assumed to be a calculated insult, and he was granted an earldom (which was then the traditional rank for a former prime minister) after reminding Macmillan that he had already been offered one by the Queen.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_609–10_175-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_pp_609–10-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> He entered the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> as the <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Avon" title="Earl of Avon">Earl of Avon</a> in 1961.<sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In retirement, Lord Avon, as he became, lived in 'Rose Bower' by the banks of the <a href="/wiki/River_Ebble" title="River Ebble">River Ebble</a> in <a href="/wiki/Broad_Chalke" title="Broad Chalke">Broad Chalke</a>, Wiltshire. Starting in 1961, he bred a herd of 60 <a href="/wiki/Hereford_(cattle)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hereford (cattle)">Herefordshire cattle</a> (one of whom was called "Churchill") until a further decline in his health forced him to sell them in 1975.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> In 1968, he bought <a href="/wiki/Alvediston_Manor" title="Alvediston Manor">Alvediston Manor</a>, where he lived until his death in 1977.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In July 1962, Lord Avon made front-page news by commenting that "Mr Selwyn Lloyd has been horribly treated" when the latter was dismissed as Chancellor in the reshuffle known as the "<a href="/wiki/Night_of_the_Long_Knives_(1962)" title="Night of the Long Knives (1962)">Night of the Long Knives</a>". In August 1962, at a dinner party, he had a "slanging match" with <a href="/wiki/Nigel_Birch,_Baron_Rhyl" title="Nigel Birch, Baron Rhyl">Nigel Birch</a>, who as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Air" title="Secretary of State for Air">Secretary of State for Air</a> had not wholeheartedly supported the Suez Invasion.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> In 1963, Lord Avon initially favoured Hailsham for the Conservative leadership but then supported <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Douglas-Home</a> as a compromise candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p611_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p611-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From 1945 to 1973, Lord Avon was <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_(education)" title="Chancellor (education)">Chancellor</a> of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Birmingham" title="University of Birmingham">University of Birmingham</a>. In a television interview in 1966, he called on the United States to halt its bombing of <a href="/wiki/North_Vietnam" title="North Vietnam">North Vietnam</a> to concentrate on developing a peace plan "that might conceivably be acceptable to <a href="/wiki/Hanoi" title="Hanoi">Hanoi</a>." The bombing of North Vietnam, he argued, would never settle the conflict in <a href="/wiki/South_Vietnam" title="South Vietnam">South Vietnam</a>. "On the contrary," he declared, "bombing creates a sort of David and Goliath complex in any country that has to suffer—as we had to, and as I suspect the Germans had to, in the last war."<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_96-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Lord Avon sat for extensive interviews for the famed multi-part <a href="/wiki/Thames_Television" title="Thames Television">Thames Television</a> production, <i><a href="/wiki/The_World_at_War_(TV_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="The World at War (TV series)">The World at War</a></i>, which was first broadcast in 1973. He also featured frequently in <a href="/wiki/Marcel_Oph%C3%BCls" class="mw-redirect" title="Marcel Ophüls">Marcel Ophüls</a>' 1969 documentary <i><a href="/wiki/The_Sorrow_and_the_Pity" title="The Sorrow and the Pity">Le chagrin et la pitié</a></i>, discussing the <a href="/wiki/German_occupation_of_France_in_World_War_II" class="mw-redirect" title="German occupation of France in World War II">occupation of France</a> in a wider geopolitical context. He spoke impeccable, if accented, French.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Avon's occasional articles and his early 1970s television appearance were an exception to an almost total retirement.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> He seldom appeared in public, unlike other former prime ministers, e.g. <a href="/wiki/James_Callaghan" title="James Callaghan">James Callaghan</a> who commented frequently on current affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p_249_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p_249-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> He was even accidentally omitted from a list of Conservative prime ministers by <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a> when she became Conservative leader in 1975, although she later went out of her way to establish relations with Lord Avon, and later, his widow.<sup id="cite_ref-Rothwell_1992,_p_249_183-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rothwell_1992,_p_249-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> In retirement, he was highly critical of regimes such as <a href="/wiki/Sukarno" title="Sukarno">Sukarno</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Indonesia" title="Indonesia">Indonesia</a> which confiscated assets belonging to their former colonial rulers, and appears to have reverted somewhat to the right-wing views which he had espoused in the 1920s.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memoirs">Memoirs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Memoirs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In retirement, Lord Avon corresponded with Selwyn Lloyd, co-ordinating the release of information and with which writers they would agree to speak and when. Rumours that Britain had colluded with France and Israel appeared, albeit in garbled form, as early as 1957. By the 1970s they had agreed that Lloyd would only tell his version of the story after Avon's death (in the event, Lloyd would outlive Lord Avon by a year, struggling with terminal illness to complete his own memoirs).<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In retirement, Lord Avon was particularly bitter that Eisenhower had initially indicated British and French troops should be allowed to remain around <a href="/wiki/Port_Said" title="Port Said">Port Said</a>, only for the US ambassador <a href="/wiki/Henry_Cabot_Lodge,_Jr" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Cabot Lodge, Jr">Henry Cabot Lodge, Jr</a>, to press for an immediate withdrawal at the UN, thereby rendering the operation a complete failure. Avon felt the Eisenhower administration's unexpected opposition was hypocritical in light of the <a href="/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1953 Iranian coup d&#39;état">1953 Iranian coup d'état</a> and the <a href="/wiki/1954_Guatemalan_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1954 Guatemalan coup d&#39;état">1954 Guatemalan coup d'état</a>.
</p><p>The Earl of Avon published three volumes of political memoirs, in which he denied that there had been any collusion with France and Israel. Like Churchill, Lord Avon relied heavily on the ghost-writing of young researchers, whose drafts he would sometimes toss angrily into the flowerbeds outside his study. One of them was the young <a href="/wiki/David_Dilks" title="David Dilks">David Dilks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p611_180-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p611-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his view, American Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles" title="John Foster Dulles">John Foster Dulles</a>, whom he particularly disliked, was responsible for the ill fate of the Suez adventure. In an October press conference, barely three weeks before the fighting began, Dulles had coupled the Suez Canal issue with colonialism, and his statement infuriated Eden and much of the UK as well. "The dispute over Nasser's seizure of the canal," wrote Eden, "had, of course, nothing to do with colonialism, but was concerned with international rights." He added that "if the United States had to defend her treaty rights in the <a href="/wiki/Panama_Canal" title="Panama Canal">Panama Canal</a>, she would not regard such action as colonialism."<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> His lack of candour further diminished his standing, and a principal concern in his later years was trying to rebuild his reputation that was severely damaged by Suez, sometimes taking legal action to protect his viewpoint.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lord Avon faulted the United States for forcing him to withdraw, but he took credit for United Nations action in patrolling the <a href="/wiki/Borders_of_Israel#Border_with_Egypt" title="Borders of Israel">Israeli-Egyptian borders</a>. Eden said of the invasion, "Peace at any price has never averted war. We must not repeat the mistakes of the pre-war years, by behaving as though the enemies of peace and order are armed with only good intentions." Recalling the incident in a 1967 interview, Lord Avon declared, "I am still unrepentant about Suez. People never look at what would have happened if we had done nothing. There is a parallel with the 1930s. If you allow people to break agreements with impunity, the appetite grows to feed on such things. I don't see what other we ought to have done. One cannot dodge. It is hard to act rather than dodge."<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_96-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> In his 1967 interview (which he stipulated would not be used until after his death), Avon acknowledged secret dealings with the French and "intimations" of the Israeli attack. He insisted, however, that "the joint enterprise and the preparations for it were justified in the light of the wrongs it [the Anglo-French invasion] was designed to prevent." "I have no apologies to offer," Eden declared.<sup id="cite_ref-nytimes.com_96-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes.com-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the time of his retirement, Eden had been short of money, although he was paid a £100,000 advance for his memoirs by <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i>, with any profit over this amount to be split between himself and the newspaper. By 1970, they had brought him £185,000 (around £3,000,000 at 2014 prices), leaving him a wealthy man for the first time in his life. Towards the end of his life, he published a personal memoir of his early life, <i>Another World</i> (1976).<sup id="cite_ref-measuringworth.com_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-measuringworth.com-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relationships">Relationships</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Relationships">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On 5 November 1923, shortly before his election to Parliament, he married <a href="/wiki/Beatrice_Beckett" title="Beatrice Beckett">Beatrice Beckett</a>, who was then eighteen.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> They had three sons: Simon Gascoigne (1924–1945), Robert, who died fifteen minutes after being born in October 1928, and <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Eden,_2nd_Earl_of_Avon" title="Nicholas Eden, 2nd Earl of Avon">Nicholas</a> (1930–1985).<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The marriage was not a success, with both parties apparently conducting affairs. By the mid-1930s his diaries seldom mention Beatrice.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p158_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p158-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> The marriage finally broke up under the strain of the loss of their son Simon, who was killed in action with the RAF in Burma in 1945. His plane was reported "missing in action" on 23 June and found on 16 July; Eden did not want the news to be public until after the election result on 26 July, to avoid claims of "making political capital" from it.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1946 and 1950, whilst separated from his wife, Eden conducted an open affair with Dorothy, Countess Beatty, the wife of <a href="/wiki/David_Beatty,_2nd_Earl_Beatty" title="David Beatty, 2nd Earl Beatty">David, Earl Beatty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden was the great-great-grandnephew of author <a href="/wiki/Emily_Eden" title="Emily Eden">Emily Eden</a> and in 1947, wrote an introduction to her novel <i>The Semi-Attached Couple</i> (1860).<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1950, Eden and Beatrice were finally divorced, and in 1952, he married Churchill's niece <a href="/wiki/Clarissa_Eden" title="Clarissa Eden">Clarissa Spencer-Churchill</a> (1920–2021), a nominal Roman Catholic who was fiercely criticised by Catholic writer <a href="/wiki/Evelyn_Waugh" title="Evelyn Waugh">Evelyn Waugh</a> for marrying a divorced man.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health_issues">Health issues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Health issues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Eden had a stomach ulcer, exacerbated by overwork, as early as the 1920s.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> He also had gallstones, requiring surgery to remove the gallbladder (<a href="/wiki/Cholecystectomy" title="Cholecystectomy">cholecystectomy</a>). The physician consulted at the time was the Royal Physician, <a href="/wiki/Horace_Evans,_1st_Baron_Evans" title="Horace Evans, 1st Baron Evans">Sir Horace Evans</a>. Three surgeons were recommended and Eden chose the one that had previously performed his <a href="/wiki/Appendectomy" title="Appendectomy">appendectomy</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Basil_Hume" title="John Basil Hume">John Basil Hume</a>, surgeon from <a href="/wiki/St_Bartholomew%27s_Hospital" title="St Bartholomew&#39;s Hospital">St Bartholomew's Hospital</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Kune_387-402_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kune_387-402-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> During the open cholecystectomy on 12 April 1953, in London, United Kingdom, it is thought that the <a href="/wiki/Common_bile_duct" title="Common bile duct">common bile duct</a> was damaged, leaving Eden susceptible to recurrent infections, <a href="/wiki/Biliary_obstruction" class="mw-redirect" title="Biliary obstruction">biliary obstruction</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Liver_failure" title="Liver failure">liver failure</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>Eden suffered from <a href="/wiki/Cholangitis" class="mw-redirect" title="Cholangitis">cholangitis</a>, an abdominal infection which became so agonising that he was admitted to hospital in 1956 with a temperature reaching 106&#160;°F (41&#160;°C). He was re-operated in London in an attempt to correct the injury with placement of a surgical drain. He suffered further with symptoms of biliary obstruction and required further revisional surgery on three more occasions in <a href="/wiki/Boston" title="Boston">Boston</a>, <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts" title="Massachusetts">Massachusetts</a> to treat recurrent stricturing of the right hepatic duct.<sup id="cite_ref-Owen_387–402_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen_387–402-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Kune_387-402_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kune_387-402-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was also prescribed <a href="/wiki/Benzedrine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benzedrine">Benzedrine</a>, the wonder drug of the 1950s. Regarded then as a harmless <a href="/wiki/Stimulant" title="Stimulant">stimulant</a>, it belongs to the family of drugs called <a href="/wiki/Amphetamines" class="mw-redirect" title="Amphetamines">amphetamines</a>, and at that time they were prescribed and used in a very casual way. Among the side effects of Benzedrine are insomnia, restlessness, and mood swings, all of which Eden suffered during the Suez Crisis; indeed, earlier in his premiership he complained of being kept awake at night by the sound of motor scooters,<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> being unable to sleep more than 5 hours per night or sometimes waking up at 3 am.<sup id="cite_ref-Owen_387–402_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen_387–402-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> Eden's drug regimen is now commonly agreed to have been a part of the reason for his bad judgment while prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The Thorpe biography, however, denied Eden's abuse of Benzedrine, stating that the allegations were "untrue, as is made clear by Eden's medical records at <a href="/wiki/University_of_Birmingham" title="University of Birmingham">Birmingham University</a>, not yet [at the time] available for research".<sup id="cite_ref-Thorpe2003_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Thorpe2003-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The resignation document written by Eden for release to the Cabinet on 9 January 1957 admitted his dependence on stimulants while denying that they had affected his judgement during the Suez crisis in the autumn of 1956. "... I have been obliged to increase the drugs [taken after the "bad abdominal operations"] considerably and also increase the stimulants necessary to counteract the drugs. This has finally had an adverse effect on my precarious inside," he wrote. However, in his book <i>The Suez Affair</i> (1966), historian <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Thomas,_Baron_Thomas_of_Swynnerton" title="Hugh Thomas, Baron Thomas of Swynnerton">Hugh Thomas</a>, quoted by <a href="/wiki/David_Owen" title="David Owen">David Owen</a>, claimed that Eden had revealed to a colleague that he was "practically living on Benzedrine" at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Owen_387–402_198-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen_387–402-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In all, at different points, but mostly simultaneously, he took a combination of <a href="/wiki/Barbiturate" title="Barbiturate">sedatives</a>, <a href="/wiki/Opioids" class="mw-redirect" title="Opioids">opioid painkillers</a> and corresponding stimulants to counteract their depressant effects; these included <a href="/wiki/Promazine" title="Promazine">Promazine</a> (a strongly sedative <a href="/wiki/Antipsychotic" title="Antipsychotic">antipsychotic</a> Eden used to induce sleep and counteract the stimulants he took), <a href="/wiki/Dextroamphetamine" title="Dextroamphetamine">Dextroamphetamine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sodium_Amytal" class="mw-redirect" title="Sodium Amytal">Sodium Amytal</a> (a <a href="/wiki/Barbiturate" title="Barbiturate">barbiturate</a> sedative), <a href="/wiki/Secobarbital" title="Secobarbital">Secobarbital</a> (a <a href="/wiki/Barbiturate" title="Barbiturate">barbiturate</a> sedative), <a href="/wiki/Vitamin_B12" title="Vitamin B12">Vitamin B12</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pethidine" title="Pethidine">Pethidine</a> (a unique opioid painkiller thought at the time to have the property of relaxing the bile ducts which is now known to be inaccurate<sup id="cite_ref-Latta_2002_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Latta_2002-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>).<sup id="cite_ref-Owen_387–402_198-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Owen_387–402-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_illness_and_death">Final illness and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Final illness and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden,_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg/220px-Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg/330px-Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg/440px-Tomb_of_Anthony_Eden%2C_Earl_of_Avon_1897-1977_-_geograph.org.uk_-_314379.jpg 2x" data-file-width="640" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Tomb at St Mary's church, <a href="/wiki/Alvediston" title="Alvediston">Alvediston</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wiltshire" title="Wiltshire">Wiltshire</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In December 1976, Lord Avon, as Eden now was, felt well enough to travel with his wife to the United States to spend Christmas and New Year with <a href="/wiki/W._Averell_Harriman" title="W. Averell Harriman">Averell</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pamela_Harriman" title="Pamela Harriman">Pamela Harriman</a>; however, after reaching the States, his health rapidly deteriorated. Prime Minister James Callaghan arranged for an <a href="/wiki/Royal_Air_Force" title="Royal Air Force">RAF</a> plane that was already in America to divert to <a href="/wiki/Miami" title="Miami">Miami</a>, to fly Avon home.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
