</p><p>Lord Avon died from metastatic <a href="/wiki/Prostate_cancer" title="Prostate cancer">carcinoma of the prostate</a> to bones and mediastinal nodes<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> at his home, <a href="/wiki/Alvediston_Manor" title="Alvediston Manor">Alvediston Manor</a> in Wiltshire, on 14 January 1977, aged 79.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> He was survived by Clarissa.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> His will was proven on <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick%27s_Day" title="Saint Patrick&#39;s Day">St. Patrick's Day</a>, 17 March, with his estate amounting to £92,900 (equivalent to £614,039 in 2021).<sup id="cite_ref-inflation-UK_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inflation-UK-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was buried in St Mary's churchyard at <a href="/wiki/Alvediston" title="Alvediston">Alvediston</a>, Wiltshire, just three miles upstream from 'Rose Bower', at the source of the River Ebble. Lord Avon's papers are housed at the University of Birmingham Special Collections.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At his death, Avon was the last surviving member of Churchill's <a href="/wiki/Churchill_war_ministry" title="Churchill war ministry">War Cabinet</a>. Avon's surviving son, <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Eden,_2nd_Earl_of_Avon" title="Nicholas Eden, 2nd Earl of Avon">Nicholas Eden, 2nd Earl of Avon</a> (1930–1985), known as Viscount Eden from 1961 to 1977, was also a politician and a minister in the Margaret Thatcher government until his death from <a href="/wiki/AIDS" class="mw-redirect" title="AIDS">AIDS</a> at the age of 54.<sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Eden was well-mannered, well-groomed, and good-looking. This image gave him huge popular support throughout his political life, but some contemporaries felt he was merely a superficial person lacking any deeper convictions.
</p><p>That view was enforced by his very <a href="/wiki/Pragmatism" title="Pragmatism">pragmatic</a> approach to politics. <a href="/wiki/Sir_Oswald_Mosley" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Oswald Mosley">Sir Oswald Mosley</a>, for example, said he never understood why Eden was so strongly pushed by the Tory party, as he felt that Eden's abilities were very much inferior to those of Harold Macmillan and <a href="/wiki/Oliver_Stanley" title="Oliver Stanley">Oliver Stanley</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> In 1947, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Crossman" title="Richard Crossman">Dick Crossman</a> called Eden "that peculiarly British type, the idealist without conviction".<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>US Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Dean_Acheson" title="Dean Acheson">Dean Acheson</a> regarded Eden as a quite old-fashioned amateur in politics, typical of the British Establishment.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, Soviet Leader <a href="/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev" title="Nikita Khrushchev">Nikita Khrushchev</a> commented that until his Suez adventure Eden had been "in the top world class".<sup id="cite_ref-213" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden was heavily influenced by Stanley Baldwin when he first entered Parliament. After earlier combative beginnings, he cultivated a low-key speaking style that relied heavily on rational argument and consensus-building, rather than rhetoric and party point-scoring, which was often highly effective in the House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> However, he was not always an effective public speaker, and his parliamentary performances sometimes disappointed many of his followers, such as after his resignation from Neville Chamberlain's government. Winston Churchill once even commented on one of Eden's speeches that the latter had used every <a href="/wiki/Clich%C3%A9" title="Cliché">cliché</a> except "<a href="/wiki/1_John" class="mw-redirect" title="1 John">God is love</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-Time-man_who_waited_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Time-man_who_waited-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> That was deliberate since Eden often struck out original phrases from speech drafts and replaced them with clichés.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden's inability to express himself clearly is often attributed to shyness and lack of self-confidence. Eden is known to have been much more direct in meeting with his secretaries and advisers than in cabinet meetings and public speeches and sometimes tended to become enraged and behave "like a child",<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> only to regain his temper within a few minutes.<sup id="cite_ref-Dutton_3-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dutton-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Many who worked for him remarked that he was "two men": one charming, erudite, and hard-working and the other petty and prone to temper tantrums during which he would insult his subordinates.<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As prime minister, Eden was notorious for telephoning ministers and newspaper editors from 6 a.m. onward. Rothwell wrote that even before Suez, the telephone had become "a drug": "During the Suez Crisis Eden's telephone mania exceeded all bounds".<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Eden was notoriously "unclubbable" and offended Churchill by declining to join <a href="/wiki/The_Other_Club" title="The Other Club">The Other Club</a>. He also declined honorary membership in the <a href="/wiki/Athenaeum_Club,_London" title="Athenaeum Club, London">Athenaeum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rhodes_James_1986,_p158_190-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rhodes_James_1986,_p158-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> However, he maintained friendly relations with Opposition MPs; for example, <a href="/wiki/George_Thomas,_1st_Viscount_Tonypandy" title="George Thomas, 1st Viscount Tonypandy">George Thomas</a> received a kind two-page letter from Eden on learning that his stepfather had died.<sup id="cite_ref-219" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Eden was a Trustee of the <a href="/wiki/National_Gallery" title="National Gallery">National Gallery</a> (in succession to MacDonald) between 1935 and 1949. He also had a deep knowledge of Persian poetry and of Shakespeare and would bond with anybody who could display similar knowledge.<sup id="cite_ref-220" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rothwell wrote that although Eden was capable of acting with ruthlessness, for instance over <a href="/wiki/Repatriation_of_Cossacks_after_World_War_II" title="Repatriation of Cossacks after World War II">the repatriation of the Cossacks</a> in 1945, his main concern was to avoid being seen as "an appeaser", such as over the Soviet reluctance to accept a democratic Poland in October 1944. Like many people, Eden convinced himself that his past actions were more consistent than they had in fact been.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Recent biographies put more emphasis on Eden's achievements in foreign policy and perceive him to have held deep convictions regarding world peace and security as well as a strong social conscience.<sup id="cite_ref-RRJames_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RRJames-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Rhodes James applied to Eden Churchill's famous verdict on <a href="/wiki/George_Curzon,_1st_Marquess_Curzon_of_Kedleston" title="George Curzon, 1st Marquess Curzon of Kedleston">Lord Curzon</a> (in <i><a href="/wiki/Great_Contemporaries" title="Great Contemporaries">Great Contemporaries</a></i>): "The morning had been golden; the noontime was bronze; and the evening lead. But all was solid, and each was polished until it shone after its fashion".<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Both <a href="/wiki/Eden_Court,_Leamington_Spa" title="Eden Court, Leamington Spa">Eden Court, Leamington Spa</a>, built in 1960, and Sir Anthony Eden Way, <a href="/wiki/Warwick" title="Warwick">Warwick</a>, built in the 2000s, are named in his honour.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cultural_depictions">Cultural depictions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Cultural depictions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_British_prime_ministers#Anthony_Eden" class="mw-redirect" title="Cultural depictions of British prime ministers">Cultural depictions of British prime ministers §&#160;Anthony Eden</a></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Archives">Archives</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Archives">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Personal and political papers of Anthony Eden and papers of the Eden family can be found at the Cadbury Research Library, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Birmingham" title="University of Birmingham">University of Birmingham</a> in the Avon Papers collection.<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> A collection of letters and other papers relating to Anthony Eden can also be found at the Cadbury Research Library, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Birmingham" title="University of Birmingham">University of Birmingham</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><br />
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Memoirs_2">Memoirs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Memoirs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>Another World</i>. London. Doubleday, 1976. Covers early life.</li>
<li><i>The Eden Memoirs: Facing the Dictators</i>. London. Cassell, 1962. Covers early career and first period as Foreign Secretary, to 1938.</li>
<li><i>The Eden Memoirs: the Reckoning</i>. London. Cassell, 1965. Covers 1938–1945.</li>
<li><i>The Eden Memoirs: Full Circle</i>. London. Cassell, 1960. Covers postwar career.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Arms">Arms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Anthony_Eden&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Arms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of Anthony Eden
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<td style="width: 220px;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Avon_Achievement.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Avon_Achievement.png/220px-Avon_Achievement.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Avon_Achievement.png/330px-Avon_Achievement.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Avon_Achievement.png/440px-Avon_Achievement.png 2x" data-file-width="708" data-file-height="710" /></a></span>
</td>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>A dexter arm in armour embowed couped at the shoulder Proper the hand grasping a Garb also Proper.</dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Gules on a chevron Argent between three garbs Or banded Vert as many escallops Sable</dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>On the dexter side a leopard guardant Or resting the sinister hind paw on a garb Or banded Vert and on the sinister side a like leopard resting the dexter hind paw on a similar garb.</dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd>Si Sit Prudentia (If There Be But Prudence)</dd>
<dt>Orders</dt>
<dd>Order of the Garter (not pictured)</dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>

<li id="cite_note-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-75">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">This was the speech in which Churchill declared "Thank God for the French Army" and in which he stated that Ramsay MacDonald had "more than any other man, the gift of compressing the largest number of words into the smallest amount of thought". 

Although Churchill compared Eden's planned trip to see Mussolini to the <a href="/wiki/Henry_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Henry IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Holy Roman Emperor</a>'s trip to <a href="/wiki/Canossa" title="Canossa">Canossa</a>, they had a friendly drink afterwards. [Rhodes James 1986, pp. 126–7]</span>
